#3007. Comcast vs. Walt Disney: Which Media Stock Is a Better Buy?

Comcast's cash machine and Disney's prized franchises pull in opposite directions, and the valuation gap between them is hard to ignore.

By Jack Delaney – Updated Nov 22, 2026 at 10:00PM EST

Is the future of media found in the cables under the street or the stories on the screen? Investors often choose between Comcast (CMCSA +0.16%) and Walt Disney (DIS -1.89%) for diversified exposure.

Comcast operates as a backbone of modern connectivity while diversifying into world-class entertainment and theme parks. Disney remains the gold standard for intellectual property, using its content library to fuel a massive ecosystem of streaming and travel. Comparing them helps clarify whether you prefer reliable utility-like cash flow or brand-driven growth.

Comcast provides broadband, wireless, and video services to millions of customers, largely under its Xfinity and Comcast Business brands. As one of the prominent media stocks, it also owns NBCUniversal, which produces television and film content and operates Universal theme parks. The company maintains critical partnerships with providers like Verizon and T-Mobile to support its expanding mobile phone service segment.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $123 billion, which represents a slight decrease of approximately 0.2% compared to the prior year. Net income for this period was roughly $11.3 billion.

Walt Disney focuses on its entertainment, sports, and experiences segments to drive global engagement. The company leverages iconic franchises through Disney+ and Hulu while managing a massive portfolio of theme parks and cruise lines. It also holds strategic stakes in other platforms, including a 70% interest in FuboTV (FUBO -11.94%) and a 37% interest in JioStar in India.

Comcast faces heavy competition from wireline and wireless broadband providers like Charter Communications (CHTR -3.34%). The continued rise of streaming services threatens the profitability of its linear network agreements and traditional video packages. Furthermore, technological shifts such as generative artificial intelligence and a $117.5 million data breach settlement represent significant operational hurdles.

Walt Disney has an intense rivalry for viewers and advertising dollars and competes with tech giants like Amazon (AMZN -4.10%) and Apple (AAPL -1.90%). Operational risks include the potential for labor strikes among park employees or entertainment guilds, as well as service blackouts during contract negotiations. Additionally, the company must manage the volatility of consumer spending on high-end vacations and cruise ship voyages.

Comcast trades at a significant discount based on its forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio, which compares the stock price to future earnings estimates, and its price-to-sales (P/S) ratio, which measures market value against sales over the past twelve months.

Deciding between Disney and Comcast really comes down to what you're looking to accomplish with your portfolio, as well as your risk tolerance. That said, between the two, I would lean toward owning Comcast over Disney.

One of the main reasons is that investors seem to be taking on less expectation risk with Comcast than with Disney. In the third quarter of 2026, Comcast generated $4.2 billion in free cash flow, giving it significant flexibility amid considerable uncertainty in the markets and the economy. While still impressive, Disney's free cash flow of $3 billion in its third quarter is noticeably less.

For Comcast, its broadband business has some issues, but its wireless segment is growing quickly and could offset weakness in that division if it continues to expand.

Disney may be a more exciting investment and has more stock price growth potential. Still, in this kind of market, I'd rather own the stock that generates more free cash flow and passes it on to its shareholders through a dividend that yields 5.3%.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased today, but borrowing costs are still expensive for many home buyers.

Mortgage rates dropped today, but anything above 7% still puts pressure on home buyers’ budgets.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.24% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and three basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

A significant drop is eye-catching, but you should consider mortgage interest rates' overall direction, not just what's going on today. While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Next week, the Nerds are waiting for the Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price index, due out Wednesday. It's the Federal Reserve's preferred measure of inflation, so investors will be watching closely. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but lenders anticipate its decisions, so mortgage rates often move before the Fed acts. Stubbornly high inflation is a big reason why we’re seeing upward pressure on mortgage rates right now.

If PCE shows inflation comes in hotter than expected, mortgage rates could climb as markets anticipate further Fed rate hikes. (An increase to the federal funds rate is already widely expected at the Fed’s Dec. 8-9 meeting.) A cooler reading could ease some pressure on mortgage rates for now.

I’m not getting my hopes up too high for meaningfully lower inflation, though. Last week's Producer Price Index showed wholesale inflation climbed 6.5% over the past year — its fastest pace in four years. Wholesale inflation measures the price businesses pay for things. When businesses’ costs go up, they could pass those along to consumers in the form of higher prices — keeping the flames of inflation alive.

Whether Wednesday's PCE brings relief or another helping of bad inflation news, we'll know before the turkey hits the table on Thursday.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.74% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

» Stay informed: Check out NerdWallet's mortgage news hub for all our latest coverage.

#2998. Chegg: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Chegg's push into student employability and corporate skilling is the piece to watch, since it decides whether revenue can rebuild once the legacy homework business fades.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 22, 2026 at 10:12PM EST

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 3, 2026, Chegg (CHGG -5.30%) announced that it has fully repaid its debt and completed a balance sheet reset. The company is shedding its declining legacy homework help business to focus on student employability and career skilling. That pivot determines whether the business can rebuild sustainable revenue. Three things management said on the call stand out.

Management trimmed expenses to keep the business profitable on an adjusted EBITDA basis, which measures core operating earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization. Paying off all remaining debt leaves the company with cash holdings to reinvest into new product lines.

Chegg launched a job platform on internships.com to capture students seeking career placement, partnering directly with universities through business-to-institution pilots. Helping colleges demonstrate career outcomes for graduates creates a direct institutional channel for student growth.

Expanding corporate skilling allows the business to deliver enterprise training packages through third-party platforms in business-to-business arrangements. Broadening these partner channels helps diversify revenue beyond individual consumer subscriptions.

Legacy academic declines are stabilizing while new skilling and employability launches pick up traction. Chegg relies on operational efficiency, reduced capital expenditures (CapEx), and artificial intelligence (AI) tools to protect cash flow as it tracks user engagement.

Management continues to evaluate targeted acquisitions alongside organic platform enhancements to accelerate growth.

#2989. College Scholarship Breakdown: Types, Sponsors and Eligibility at a Glance

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Scholarships vary in what they pay for and who can apply. Understanding the differences can save you time during your application process.

Scholarships have become a standard part of applying for college. You can learn about scholarships in a variety of ways, but looking through an online database of available scholarships — the most common way — can be overwhelming.

Before you go searching for a scholarship, it's important to understand the types of scholarships available and how to identify the ones that give you the best chance of winning, which can save you time and potentially earn you more funding. Here’s what you need to know about scholarships to decide which ones are best for you.

Scholarships are often funded by three groups: private organizations, institutions like universities, and government agencies. None is better than the other when it comes to awarding funds.

Nonprofit organizations and clubs that are looking for students either majoring in or researching certain fields, or who have experience volunteering in a specific field.

Companies that want to support all students or only fund those majoring in a field that relates to the companies’ industries.

Financial institutions, such as banks or credit unions, that want to support their community or their members.

Individuals or families that are funding private scholarships, usually in memory of a loved one.

Universities and colleges that are offering funds to students who attend their campuses.

Research organizations that want to fund students who will research in their areas of interest and further their causes.

These scholarships generally are provided by local, state or federal government agencies for a variety of reasons. Despite being funded by a government agency, the scholarships do not require you to pay back the money — unlike federal student loans.

Some scholarships pay for anything related to college expenses, including tuition, room and board, books and supplies, and more. Other scholarships limit what you can pay for with the funds, such as only tuition or tuition along with books. It’s important to look at this restriction when applying for scholarships to ensure the funds will go toward what you need to cover.

Merit scholarships require you to show excellence or achievements in your schoolwork, through talents and abilities, and even leadership or service work. Because these types of awards can be highly competitive, merit scholarships might require more than just achievements, such as volunteer or leadership experience. This means merit-based scholarships can overlap with other types of scholarship categories. Here are some examples of merit-based scholarships:

Merit scholarships often require you to have earned a minimum grade point average (GPA). The minimum GPA depends on the scholarship but often ranges from 2.0 to 4.0, with more competitive scholarships usually requiring higher GPAs. Scholarships with lower GPA minimums are more likely to consider additional factors, such as financial need.

Some merit scholarships might require a minimum ACT or SAT score instead of a GPA — or in addition to a minimum GPA.

Merit scholarships might also require applicants to have participated in activities outside of their schoolwork. This is common for scholarships that require membership in a certain group or organization. For example, membership in agricultural clubs, such as 4-H or FFA, and scouting groups, such as the National Eagle Scout Association, appear in scholarship requirements.

Some scholarships emphasize volunteering or community service and only consider applicants who have that experience. Applications vary in how detailed their requirements can be. Some scholarships want only a general description of where and how you’ve volunteered, and others might require experience with a service project that shows more leadership experience or time volunteering with a certain type of organization.

Scholarships that are awarded to students who can’t afford college are called need-based scholarships.

Unmet financial need is the difference between what it will cost you to attend college and what you can afford to pay. Because calculating how much you can afford is a complicated process, many scholarships refer to your FAFSA to document your financial need.

Some scholarship requirements may be as straightforward as demonstrating that you have financial need, but some scholarships might have income limits. However, income restrictions are less common.

Athletic scholarships are for — you guessed it — athletes. They are usually offered by colleges and universities to recruit athletes onto their collegiate teams and can be competitive. Which sports have scholarships available and the scholarship amounts vary by college.

Even though the main criterion for an athletic scholarship is being good at your sport, you’ll likely still need to show that you can keep up with your schoolwork. Most athletic scholarships require students to earn a minimum GPA or test scores to remain eligible while they’re in college.

Some scholarships are designated for students majoring in a general field, such as science or education, but others can be for very specific areas of study. For example, a scholarship for students majoring in a natural resource field could include majors in biology, ecology, wildlife biology, conservation education, forestry, soils and hydrology. However, a stricter scholarship might state that students have to major in more specific topics, such as biochemistry or microbial ecology.

Scholarships are available for active and former members of the military and members of their families. In addition to federal funds available for veterans to attend college, private organizations and some universities have scholarships for veterans to offset the cost of a degree.

Which family members can apply for funds depends on the individual scholarship. For example, some scholarships extend to children or spouses of veterans, but some go as far in a family tree as great-grandchildren.

Veteran- and military-focused scholarships might require that applicants or their family members enlisted in a certain branch, served during a particular time or conflict, or meet a certain rank or status such as receiving disability. Scholarships might also require that the applicant be a wartime veteran or family member of a wartime veteran; not all veterans meet this status.

Scholarships are also awarded to people who identify with a certain group. Common examples of identity-based scholarships include those for people who have disabilities, belong to certain ethnic or religious groups, identify as LGBTQIA+ or are first-generation students. Some identity-based scholarships require applicants to meet several criteria, such as belonging to more than one identity group.

Outside of what would be considered standard DEI groups, there are scholarships for applicants who identify with less common groups, such as veganism, homelessness and model railroad builders.

The best scholarships for you depend on your situation. For example, if you are a skilled athlete with a good academic record, going for an athletic scholarship might give you the most funding. On the other hand, if you’re going to research a topic that isn’t common, looking for scholarships that support your research topic might be your best bet because competition could be lower.

Good tactics to figure out which scholarships are worth your time include:

Think of the skills, talents, experience and identities that are unique to you. For example, being a child of a veteran immediately qualifies you for scholarships with fewer applicants than scholarships open to everyone. Likewise, if you embrace a certain ethnicity, identify with a specific group or have experience with a type of work, you might qualify for scholarships that appreciate and recognize those attributes more than the average scholarship.

Apply if you think you're eligible. Don’t apply to just a few scholarships and hope you get them. Instead, apply for as many scholarships as you can and for which you’re eligible. This increases your chances of receiving funds. Even small scholarships can make a big impact when you add several of them together.

Understand how you can use the money. If you already have a scholarship that covers only books, applying for the same type of scholarship isn’t helpful if you don’t need the extra funds. Instead, look for scholarships that cover the costs you have remaining.

Not sure where to look? Our sister site Scholarships360.org provides students with vetted scholarship opportunities. That would be a good place to start.

#2987. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

#2985. These 5 Stocks Could Double by 2028. Here's What Has to Go Right.

SpaceX, Broadcom, SentinelOne, SoundHound, and Dutch Bros have the potential to double.

By Geoffrey Seiler – Nov 23, 2026 at 12:00AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Space Exploration Technologies ›

When you're investing in individual stocks, you're always looking for a few that have the potential to double in a couple of years. Not every investment will pan out, but a few big winners will help lead to strong overall performance. Let's look at five growth stocks that have the potential to double by 2028 and what would need to happen for them to achieve that.

Despite its size, SpaceX (SPCX -7.69%) has clear potential to double in the next two years. For this to happen, it's going to have to grow rapidly and continue to hit important technological milestones, like it did when it recently caught the upper stage of its Starship rocket.

Much of SpaceX's growth in the next two years will come from its infrastructure-as-a-service business. To quickly grow this business, it's going to add a lot of compute capacity while maintaining its strong payback periods. If it can navigate permitting issues and industry bottlenecks and hit its growth targets of adding 10 gigawatts of power next year, and do that again in 2028, the stock could double.

The market is shifting toward custom artificial intelligence (AI) chips and inference, which plays right into Broadcom's (AVGO -12.19%) wheelhouse. With the stock trading at a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) multiple of just 11 times fiscal 2028 analyst estimates (ending October 2028), it has a big opportunity to double in the next two years.

For this to happen, the company is going to have to maintain solid hardware gross margins and not lose significant share in the application-specific integrated circuit (ASIC) market to cheaper competitors in future chip iterations. It's also going to have to see large customers Anthropic (ANT -8.50%) and OpenAI thrive, as much of its future projected custom chip revenue is coming from these two customers and Alphabet (GOOGL -2.70%) (GOOG -2.59%).

Trading at a huge discount to larger cybersecurity rivals while growing quickly, SentinelOne (S -4.58%) stock could easily double from continued growth and multiple expansion. Right now, the stock trades at a forward price-to-sales (P/S) multiple of 6 times 2027 estimates compared to 17.5 times for Palo Alto Networks (PANW -5.09%) and 37 times for CrowdStrike (CRWD -5.76%), so the opportunity for significant multiple expansion is there.

The company has been aggressively making moves to become a strong provider in the AI era, and if this leads to accelerating growth, the stock has a great opportunity to double. Analysts at Wedbush, meanwhile, noted after talking to vendors and resellers that customers were starting to expand with SentinelOne solutions, and the company was winning in the small and medium-sized business (SMB) and mid-market spaces due to its strong abilities and cheaper pricing.

Beaten to a pulp over the past two years, SoundHound AI (SOUN -10.31%) has the potential to see its stock double in the coming years, with its valuation down to a forward P/S multiple under 6. For this to happen, it will need to stabilize and integrate its recent LivePerson acquisition, hit its target of becoming earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) profitable in the second half of 2027, and see strong adoption of OASYS, its new voice, language intelligence, and agentic AI orchestration solution.

The ingredients are all there; it will just be a matter of execution. Right now, the market is betting SoundHound won't be able to pull this off, but if it does, the upside is enormous.

Stepping outside of the tech sector, Dutch Bros (BROS -4.01%) has one of the best opportunities in the consumer space for its stock to double over the next two years. The coffee shop operator has been delivering strong same-store sales growth while it has huge expansion opportunities. However, the stock has been beaten down over the past year, leaving it at a very attractive P/S valuation compared to rival Starbucks (SBUX -1.28%) despite its much longer growth runway.

If Dutch Bros can consistently deliver better-than-expected, same-store sales growth over the next two years while growing its store base, its stock should be able to double. Some of its initiatives to drive same-store growth include menu expansion, including food; order ahead; its loyalty program; and piloting third-party delivery.

#2976. Innodata's 2027 Outlook: AI Data Engineering Pivot Drives High-Value Lifecycle Contracts

This under-the-radar AI services stock boasts a Superscore of 82 from our Hidden Gems Primary database, part of The Motley Fool's Moneyball Database system. Here's why.

By Robert Izquierdo – Updated Nov 23, 2026 at 12:26AM EST

In the quiet town of Ridgefield Park, New Jersey, sits a company that has spent 35 years building the infrastructure for other people's technology. For most of its history, Innodata (INOD -8.91%) was a back-office service provider, the kind of company that handled mundane data tasks that big tech companies couldn't be bothered with.

Then came the generative artificial intelligence boom, and the company found itself sitting on a pile of human-annotated data that frontier model labs desperately needed. The stock, trading at $28.12 as of the week ending Nov. 20, 2026, has seen its price decline more than 40% over the past year, reflecting the market's ongoing reassessment of the company's growth trajectory and project-based revenue volatility.

Our proprietary Hidden Gems scoring system assigns Innodata an overall Superscore of 82 out of 100, placing it in the Strong category. The Superscore is an AI-powered score that evaluates a company's overall strength by combining financial performance, product market position, technological capabilities, leadership quality, and relative valuation. It represents the unification of all our scores into a single score for public companies, with five rating bands: Exceptional (90-100), Strong (75-89), Above Average (60-74), Average (40-59), and Cautious (0-39).

An 82 places Innodata in the Top ~7% of every company we score, ahead of roughly 93 out of every 100 companies we evaluate. This score is a single data-driven signal, and this research pairs the strengths with the risks so you can weigh both sides before doing more work.

Innodata maintains a high return on net tangible assets, which means the business earns outsized profits on a small base of hard physical equipment. This efficiency allows the company to turn each point of revenue growth into meaningful returns, which helps explain why investors might pay a premium for the shares despite the underlying execution risks.

The Hidden Gems Superscore reflects The Motley Fool's proprietary AI-driven evaluation of a company across product, financial, leadership, and valuation pillars as of the article date and may change over time. Performance figures are point-in-time. Past performance does not guarantee future results.

You may want to keep researching before buying if...

The Superscore is just one signal in your research process; always weigh these findings against your personal financial goals and risk tolerance before making any investment decision.

Thanks to the massive AI tailwind, Innodata is poised to see sales growth in the coming years. That's because the organizations building AI models need the software to deliver results that are accurate, safe, and reliable. Innodata is a key enabler of that outcome.

Consequently, the enterprise is now building a business pipeline with the U.S. government, including work with the Department of War and Navy. Its quality of work is of such high caliber that when AI researchers switch companies, they call Innodata to help them at the new organization.

Revenue concentration among a subset of customers is a concern, but Innodata is working to diversify. As an example of its progress here, even though it lost a project with one of its traditionally largest customers, the company still raised its full-year 2026 revenue growth guidance to 42% or more year over year, up from 40%, as new projects come online.

Innodata is also benefiting from the rise of physical AI, where it is using motion capture of human movement to train humanoid robots how to move like people. Through these numerous AI-fueled opportunities, Innodata is well positioned to see its business grow in the years ahead.

#2968. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#2959. Innodata: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Its balance sheet holds $153.7 million in cash net of prepayments and no debt, giving management room to fund research without new financing.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 23, 2026 at 12:52AM EST

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 5, 2026, Innodata (INOD -8.91%) announced revenue of $95.6 million, up 53% year over year. The data provider is shifting from one-off model labeling into recurring training infrastructure for complex artificial intelligence systems. That shift broadens its revenue base across frontier technology labs and enterprise customers. Three takeaways from the call stand out for long-term investors.

Innodata previously suffered from revenue concentration, relying heavily on a single main customer. Management is now expanding relationships with other technology giants, forecasting over $20 million in 2027 revenue from a major hyperscaler that generated just $3.1 million over the trailing 12 months.

Training robotics foundation models requires custom video and movement data set generation, since web-based data sets don't exist for physical actions. Building these specialized physical data sets allows the company to become critical recurring infrastructure as industrial and consumer robotics move toward commercial deployment.

The sudden termination of a major program by the company's historically largest customer creates a temporary headwind in the fourth quarter. Management expects to fill that $12 million revenue gap with replacement programs by the first quarter of 2027, illustrating how individual program lifecycles introduce short-term lumpiness.

Growth momentum builds as artificial intelligence spending shifts toward complex autonomous agents and physical robotics. The company holds structural advantages in proprietary data collection, custom simulation environments, and deep relationships with frontier research labs. Management is watching organic capital deployment and execution timelines across its expansion drivers.

With $153.7 million in cash net of customer prepayments and zero debt, the balance sheet gives the company full flexibility to fund internal research and development. Pipeline expansion across enterprise and government partners positions the business for continued top-line acceleration.

#2957. Nvidia Just Reported Its First Quarter Without Jensen Huang. If the AI Trade Is Cracking, Warren Buffett’s Advice Comes Down to 1 Move.

Buffett has age-old advice that can help you survive different market paradigms.

By Brett Schafer – Nov 23, 2026 at 1:00AM EST

It has been a rough few weeks for Nvidia (NVDA -14.01%) shareholders. The stock has quickly fallen 47.5% from all-time highs due to founder Jensen Huang's ouster over an affair scandal, the potential sale of computer chips that deliberately circumvented government sanctions, and looming competition in artificial intelligence (AI) compute that could erode Nvidia's once-flaunted moat.

Some investors argue that the AI trade is cracking, and that Nvidia's future will be worse than its near past. Before buying the dip in Nvidia shares, investors should heed the long-standing advice of investing legend Warren Buffett given this latest earnings result and market volatility.

Nvidia's third-quarter numbers were fantastic. Revenue grew over 100% year over year to $116 billion, driven by growth in data center computer chips to $107.6 billion in revenue. Margins were good, with trailing operating margin hitting 66%, making Nvidia one of the most profitable companies in the world. According to the price-to-earnings ratio (P/E), Nvidia trades at just 13.5x its trailing numbers. For a business growing revenue 100% year over year, this figure looks quite cheap.

That is not what investors care about. They care about future earnings, and the predictability of future earnings just got muddied in an extreme way. First, Huang is not running the business anymore. He was wheeling and dealing to secure contracts for Nvidia computer chips in recent years. Second, the United States government is investigating Nvidia for potentially selling illegal chips to third parties that then sent them to China, and there's also a Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) investigation into its accounting statements.

Plus, its customers are either teetering on the brink financially or looking to replace Nvidia chips in their data centers. The so-called neoclouds like CoreWeave and Nebius Group have plunged amid funding concerns. These make up $47.5 billion of the $107.6 billion in data center revenue. Other revenue comes from hyperscalers that are all selling or researching how to build their own computer chips to lower costs for cloud customers.

There is a lot to balance when analyzing an investment in Nvidia today. Given the trading volume in the stock, many investors have looked to buy the dip, with shares down by almost half from the highs.

Buffett's advice is to avoid such complicated situations as an investor. In fact, the saying goes that Buffett wants businesses so good that even a bad CEO could run them, because if you are going to hold a stock for multiple decades, a bad leader will show up sooner or later. Therefore, you want to invest in high-quality businesses with good defensibility that are simple to understand. Nvidia is the opposite of this.

No stock better exemplifies this strategy for Buffett than Coca-Cola. The soda brand has been a long-running holding for Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway simply because it is the leader in a profitable category. No matter what happens technologically in the coming decades, people will likely still drink Coca-Cola. The same can't be said for Nvidia chips, which is why Buffett has never made it or rapidly changing technologies a part of his portfolio.

Lastly, investors should learn from Buffett's strategy more broadly to maintain wealth over the decades. He never liked participating in thematic bull markets, whether it was the dot-com bubble, housing in the early 2000s, or artificial intelligence today. Wall Street may love hot stocks for a time, and Buffett was willing to initially look dumb for avoiding them, but there is almost always a wash-out on the other side. Choosing not to get swept up in fear of missing out (FOMO) is one of the keys to Buffett's long-term success, and that will likely be the case with Berkshire Hathaway during this AI bear market as well.

The secret to Buffett's durability was not necessarily what he bought, but what he avoided as an investor over 75 years in the market.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 719%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 192% for the S&P 500.

#2953. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2942. Retirees face a looming deadline for required withdrawals — missing it could trigger a tax penalty

Every year, millions of Americans must take required withdrawals from retirement accounts by Dec. 31.

Amid the busy holiday season, it's easy to miss the year-end deadline for required minimum distributions — a mistake that can trigger a hefty IRS penalty, experts said.

"One of the most common RMD mistakes is waiting until the last minute," said certified financial planner Alex Canellopoulos, director of wealth at Vista Capital Partners in Portland, Oregon.

Most retirees must start RMDs from pretax accounts at age 73. The withdrawal is based on the previous year's account balances on Dec. 31, your age and an IRS "life expectancy factor."

The first annual RMD deadline is April 1 of the year after you turn 73, and future withdrawals are due by Dec. 31. If you wait until April 1 for the first one, you'll need two RMDs that year.

RMDs also apply to certain inherited IRAs if the original IRA owner reached RMD age before their death.

In 2023, more than 14.8 million taxpayers age 70 or older had a traditional individual retirement account, according to the latest IRS data. The average year-end market value of the accounts was about $330,000.

Deferring RMDs until the last minute raises the likelihood of mistakes, such as overlooking certain retirement accounts or making withdrawal calculation errors, Canellopoulos said.

Some 7% of Vanguard investors at RMD age missed their yearly withdrawal in 2025, up slightly from 6.7% in 2024, according to a Vanguard analysis of about 400,000 RMD-age retirees with IRA balances published in December 2025.

Among those investors, the average missed RMD was $10,500 in 2025, Vanguard told CNBC.

Investors who don't take RMDs by the annual deadline may owe an excise tax to the IRS, worth up to 25% of the amount they should have withdrawn. For example, the excise tax could be up to $2,625 for a $10,500 missed RMD.

If investors miss a required withdrawal, they could reduce the penalty to 10% if they "timely corrected" the RMD within two years and file Form 5329, according to the IRS.

In some cases, the IRS could waive the penalty entirely due to "reasonable error" if investors take "reasonable steps" to fix the shortfall, according to the agency. Investors can file Form 5329 with a letter of explanation to request the waiver.

"If you don't ask, you're not going to get it," said certified public accountant Tim Steffen, director of advanced planning at financial services firm Baird. "I've never heard of anybody being denied the waiver."

Investors who give to charity may be skipping a specific donation strategy that can reduce or eliminate their annual RMDs, according to Lawrence Pon, a CFP and CPA based in Redwood City, California.

They can use a so-called qualified charitable distribution, or QCD, Pon said.

A QCD has a dual tax benefit: It doesn't count as taxable income and may also satisfy at least part of an investor's yearly RMD, Pon said.

The move requires a direct transfer from an investor's pretax IRA to an eligible nonprofit organization. They can donate up to $111,000 in 2026. While QCD eligibility begins at age 70½, transfers won't start counting toward RMDs until an investor reaches their RMD age.

Generally, taxpayers only get a tax break for large charitable donations if they itemize deductions on their tax returns. But QCDs still provide a tax benefit for charitable gifts when investors use the standard deduction.

QCDs "provide a real benefit if you are not itemizing [tax deductions]," said Pon.

While there's no tax deduction for QCDs, the transfer won't increase a taxpayer's adjusted gross income, or AGI. Raising AGI can cause other tax consequences, such as higher premiums for Medicare Part B and Part D.

#2940. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2939. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2936. Nvidia stock plunges 14%, wraps up longest weekly losing streak since 2008 financial crisis

The last time Wall Street was this down on Nvidia the chipmaker's flagship product was the GeForce graphics processor for high-end PC video games, and markets were reeling from the global financial crisis.

Nvidia shares tumbled 14% on Friday, sliding for a seventh straight day as investors grew increasingly concerned about life after co-founder Jensen Huang. The stock closed lower for a seventh consecutive week, the longest such stretch since 2008.

Shares of Nvidia are down 46% over the period, pushing the company's market cap down by about $2.6 trillion. The stock is now trading at roughly 8.4 times forward earnings, while Advanced Micro Devices is valued at 29 times profit for the next 12 months.

On Friday, Reuters reported that the Securities and Exchange Commission is exploring potential accounting issues at Nvidia, pushing the stock down further.

Once the most valuable company in the world, Nvidia now has a market cap of $3 trillion, putting it fourth behind Apple, Microsoft and Alphabet.

Nvidia is reeling from the removal of Huang, who resigned on Nov. 12, after multiple reported affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who was allegedly a spy.

Harvey Jones, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, said earlier this week that Huang had resigned after "disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct, standards and cultural norms."

But the chipmaker's losing streak started before Nvidia was thrown into crisis as investors worried over the sustainability of the AI buildout, particularly after the midterm elections, and emerging competition in AI chips from Google and AMD.

Even a better-than-expected earnings report, which included an increase to fiscal 2028 guidance, didn't stem the selling. Interim CEO Colette Kress, the company's longtime CFO, projected revenue growth next fiscal year of 75%, and said that customers aren't going anywhere.

On Tuesday's earnings call with analysts, Kress signed off by saying, "we've got this." In a company meeting the next day, executives told staff that it was moving its yearly review process, which can come with more stock, from March to December.

For investors, one major question is whether Nvidia can continue to innovate and sustain its commanding lead in the AI chip market without its visionary leader. Almost the entire company now is built around the AI trade, a phenomenon that began taking hold just four years ago with the launch of OpenAI's ChatGPT.

Before that, Nvidia's primary business was selling graphics cards used for video games. In mid-2008, the last time the stock was this deep into a weekly losing streak, Nvidia was generating less than $1 billion per quarter. Now it pulls in more than $1 billion per day. At the time, the entire market was in freefall due to the worst financial crisis since the Great Depression.

#2930. Steve Kerr, coaching staff tasked with burden of lifting impaired Warriors

Steve Kerr, coaching staff tasked with burden of lifting impaired Warriors originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SAN FRANCISCO – After sinking from inspirational to abominable following one cataclysmic instant, the Warriors returned home this week hoping to find their way back to mediocrity.

That, after all, was the reasonable expectation in the absence of Stephen Curry, whose oblique strain sustained 11 days ago left the Warriors, then 8-3, without their physical nerve center and emotional support sniper.

If Curry's teammates could win 50 percent of their games without him, that would defy history; the Warriors have lost 63 percent of their games when he is sidelined. They were 7-2 this season with him before the injury – splitting two games on his mandated rest nights – and they are 0-4 without him.

These were not ordinary losses but public spankings.

Until Curry returns, and there is no timeline, the burden of being competitive will get heavier on the heads of coach Steve Kerr and his staff, along with the primary players on the roster.

Can the Warriors get greater impact from veterans like Draymond Green, De'Anthony Melton and reserves Al Horford and Georges Niang? Can youngsters Yaxel Lendeborg and Brandin Podziemski, who rank behind only Curry in offensive production, recover from a forgettable road trip?

"The game is obviously harder for everybody without Steph, but especially the guys who have to take on the ballhandling and scoring role," Kerr said Saturday after practice. "I don't love that conversation, particularly for those two guys. That with Steph out, they have to do this, they have to do that. We have to do this, or we have to do that.

"If our execution isn't good, we can't expect them to make something out of nothing. If we can execute better . . . and that's what today's practice was about, and that's what the last couple of days have been about for our staff. Understanding why we're not executing better and giving our guys something to hang their hats on offensively and that's our job. That's what we've been working on."

Can't it be difficult, particularly for young players with expectations, to avoid the pitfall of believing they must fill some of the void created by a missing superstar?

"It's challenging," Podziemski conceded. "It's human nature. It's ego. It's pride. You want to showcase what you can do. That's for everybody."

Podziemski struggled to find his footing, and Lendeborg spent the entire road trip out of sorts, as if the inner "dawg" that was so visible through the first 11 games had gone astray. Aside from a 20-point second half in a 21-point loss to the sorry New Orleans Pelicans, he rarely was a presence. He clearly was affected by his inability to excel in an elevated role.

"That's why I'm (Yaxel's) coach, and that's why I talked to him today," Kerr said. "He's a rookie and he's three weeks (four weeks) into this thing. He's learning and he's growing. And it's my job to help him."

Lendeborg's intricate path to the NBA required considerable mental fortitude, which suggests he'll probably restart his engine and get back to hooping, maybe as soon as Sunday against the impressive Boston Celtics.

But navigating such circumstances while chasing potential can be tricky.

"When your best player is out, I think there's a natural instinct to look inward and say what do I need to do more of, for everybody one through 15," Podziemski said. "But when you think about it that way, it harms the team more than helps because you're putting yourself before the team. If you just think about what the team needs, no matter if Steph is in or out, whatever you're good at just bring that to the table."

Losing five consecutive games following an eight-game win streak has the entire operation trying to fix what's broken. There's no suitable replacement for what Curry brings, so it's about limiting mistakes – and there have been plenty on offense and defense – and the Warriors reminding opponents they came to play.

Not for a few possessions or even a full quarter. For all 48 minutes.

"There's no magic pill to it," Kerr said. "It's everything. It's a comprehensive game. If our defense improves, our offense will improve. But it has to start on the defensive end. We can't sit here and say we need to replace Steph's scoring, or we need to throw the ball to so-and-so. It's defense, defense, defense. Grit, competitiveness, fighting.

"Our offense will come from that. We will make some tweaks, no question, but it is it's not about the tweaks. It's about the competitive fire."

That's the surest way for the Warriors to be competitive with Curry, and it's the only way they can compete without him. Sure, they opened the season generally projected to hover around .500. Mediocrity until Jimmy Butler and Moses Moody return when the calendar flips to 2027.

Golden State's eight-game win streak raised the bar, as the Warriors looked like a playoff-caliber team. The ensuing five-game losing streak lowered the bar, as they were like one of the worst teams in the league.

As much as Curry means to this team, it has enough to climb out of the mudhole that formed with five consecutive losses an average of 23.2 points. And, yes, the rise must start with defense.

#2927. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2924. Open Thread: “The Blueprint” honors Harrison Barnes leadership and dedication

I was fortunate enough to attend the Emirates Cup game between the San Antonio Spurs and the Golden State Warriors. Not only was thirty-seven point drubbing a sight to behold, the energy in the arena was palpable. But there is something to watching the games from the comfort of my own couch. One of those is the commentary, another is the replays, and finally, there are the features.

When I finally rewatched the game on DAZN, I enjoyed this video presentation in the first act of the halftime break:

"The Blueprint" is a three-minute tribute honoring the Spurs veteran Harrison Barnes. Barnes originally came to San Antonio in a three-team trade involving the Spurs, Sacramento Kings, and Chicago Bulls. The trade sent DeMar DeRozan to Sacramento and included RaiQuan Gray, Chris Duarte, and led to Charles Bassey being waived.

Since arriving in San Antonio, Barnes has taken on a leadership role and offered a solid veteran presence to the fledgling Spurs team. He is a 2015 NBA Champion through his time with the Golden State Warriors. On the court, he's known for his consistent 3-point shooting and high basketball IQ. In the locker room, he exhibits an example of maturity and patience that lifted the team's spirits as they put the pieces together.

Away from the game, he —along with his with Brittany— have time and again displayed grace and generosity in the community. Through multiple philanthropic endeavors, the Barnes have contributed to projects involving improvements to recreational locales, helping local students afford college tuition, and treating patrons to groceries during the holidays.

Written and edited by Liam Capwell, Spurs Associate Broadcast Producer, "The Blueprint" Capwell blends footage from Spurs media day with some of Barnes best performances while being narrated by Jacob Tobey, Spurs play-by-play announcer. Capwell comes to the Spurs after studying film at Texas State, a university with penchant for recommending talented students for Spurs internship opportunities.

#2921. How China is using summits to expand its global influence

SHENZHEN, China — For a second year in a row, Chinese President Xi Jinping has brought Russian President Vladimir Putin together with another major world leader on mainland Chinese shores.

U.S. President Donald Trump had a trilateral meeting with Xi and Putin on Thursday, after the three met the prior evening during a welcome banquet for the Asia-Pacific Economic Leaders' Meeting in Shenzhen. It marked the first time they had met together in person.

In September 2025, Xi, Putin and Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi briefly laughed together for a friendly photo-op alongside an annual meeting of the Shanghai Cooperation Organization in Tianjin, China. The gathering followed easing tensions between Beijing and New Delhi.

China's "ability to engage directly with the world major powers" reflects the country's influence across the Global South and Eurasia, said Jodie Wen, postdoctoral fellow at the Center for International Security and Strategy at Tsinghua University. "China hopes to play a more constructive role in this conflict-[ridden], unstable world."

In the future, "China will hope to provide more platforms" within its borders for countries to discuss issues, she said.

Trump, Xi, Putin said they plan to boost oil production, revamp UN after trilateral talks

I watched Trump, Xi and Putin gather in China. Here are my top takeaways

Trump holds talks separately with Putin and Xi ahead of their historic trilateral meeting

Takaichi gets chilly reception in China on first Japan leader visit since 2019

Why the Middle East is watching the Trump-Xi-Putin summit

Trump arrives in China for historic meeting with Xi, Putin

China's Xi calls for removing trade and investment barriers

Putin says EU is losing industry, falling behind competition

Three things to watch as Trump, Xi and Putin meet in China

China has increasingly played the role of broker, for instance, providing the forum for restoring diplomatic ties between Saudi Arabia and Iran in 2023.

By participating in and hosting different multilateral organizations, China has stayed in the Western orbit while cultivating relations with non-Western countries. The two photo-ops last year and this year reflect only a portion of Xi's large number of meetings with different world leaders.

Xi has hosted more than 40 heads of state so far this year, including at least eight around the APEC meetings in Shenzhen this week. That's on top of receiving over 60 state visits in 2025, according to CNBC analysis of official Chinese readouts.

Domestic Chinese media have highlighted how Xi has met with many foreign leaders around the China-hosted events.

"Beijing sees these multilateral platforms as opportunities to expand its international influence and demonstrate its commitment to multilateralism, while bilateral diplomacy remains equally important," said Yue Su, principal economist for China at EIU.

But she noted that different countries increasingly pursue different geopolitical and economic priorities, making it challenging to reach consensus even through multilateral organizations.

"Against this backdrop, China's diplomatic priorities are increasingly focused on delivering tangible economic benefits through trade, investment, financial connectivity and technology cooperation," she said.

On Thursday, Xi announced at the APEC Economic Leaders' Meeting that China would provide $20 million in support for the Asia Pacific region. He also advocated for the development of an "Asia-Pacific community with a shared future," an iteration of his commonly used slogan on a "shared future" for humanity more broadly.

The Shenzhen summit can create meaningful outcomes for crisis management, as well as diplomatic space for future negotiations, Tsinghua University's Wen said. She added that it's important for leaders to come together in person in order to avoid misunderstandings and further tensions.

Trump said Thursday at the trilateral that the U.S., China and Russia are "uniquely positioned" to discuss reducing nuclear weapons at their historic meeting. A Chinese readout subsequently said the three leaders discussed Ukraine, the Middle East and the Korean Peninsula.

—CNBC's Matthew Tan contributed to this report.
