#2919. Wolves 104, Grizzlies 95: Defense Downs the Bear

Basketball is a funny thing. One night, the world is obsessed with your offense-capping defensive stalwart and the next you're holding the number one offense in all of basketball to 95 points while that aforementioned rim protector has an incredible game (and still missed some layups).

In a game in which they looked dominant throughout, the Minnesota Timberwolves taught the Memphis Grizzlies that there is joy in being a young, upstart team, but it is still incredibly hard to face off with a capable team on a random Saturday with some of your best players absent.

No, luckily, the Wolves have seemingly learned this lesson.

Minnesota's nine-point margin is significantly smaller than it likely should be after the Grizzlies outscored them by 12 in the fourth quarter. Don't be fooled, this was functionally a wire to wire victory.

The Wolves employed a bit of a platoon plan on offense tonight, spreading usage to each of the starters and allowing Jonathan Kuminga, Jaden McDaniels, and Ayo Dosunmu to all turn in impressive point totals. This Wolves team just keeps showing up and playing well in a way that could not be further from what Wolves fans are used to. Memphis leaned on Cedric Coward and Most Improved Player frontrunner Ty Jerome, but struggled to do much of anything until a garbage time run.

This was a game that put the floor of the Wolves on display more than its ceiling. This was a close win against an admittedly great team with clear weaknesses. If we're going to start there, with the two-in-one ceiling capper and floor raiser, there's only one man to talk about.

Look, the discourse surrounding Rudy Gobert does make sense. He may be the single greatest floor-raising defensive presence since Tim Duncan or Hakeem Olajuwon, but he also makes those two look like Moses combined with prime Escalade on offense.

(And yes, folks, going from being a bad catcher of the basketball to a stone-handed anomaly who is putting up 40ish% on layups is, in fact, a major regression).

Tonight was one of those nights where everything looked decent for Gobert, even if he only scored seven points. Considering the state of the timeline over the past few days – if you are lobbying for Myles Turner, you live in a 2K fanfiction – it was nice to have a reminder of what a *good* Gobert game can look like.

Anytime Minnesota has a great defensive game, everyone will place the flowers at Gobert's feet. This is, after all, exactly what he was supposed to do here. However, the hand-wave of "Gobert was great" doesn't give him his full deserved credit. Memphis did not look at the rim all game. That meant that by the time they got hot, they were in two deep of a hole to get out of.

The man may be an absolute deadbeat, have the health/food knowledge of a yule log (maybe one that's worm-eaten too), but he is an absurdly game-altering talent.

It may be impossible to replace him, and no one seems willing to give up anything of value for him. At that point, it seems the future holds a lot of floor-raising. After tonight, that sounds pretty perfect.

Five different players hit double digits for Minnesota tonight. Jaden McDaniels (25 points on 11/17 from the field), Ayo Dosunmu (19 points), Jonathan Kuminga (16 points on 6/9 shooting), LaMelo Ball (on 50% from the field after a bad stretch), and TJ Shannon Jr. each scored over 10 points, and together they provided all but 17 points in this game.

The headliner is a hyper-efficient Jaden McDaniels game, but it feels like he is destined to never truly empty the clip, instead opting to be a capable third option no matter who he is sharing the court with. Some days, he's the number three guy to Anthony Edwards and LaMelo Ball; other days, he's letting Greg Monroe initiate the offense while on a 10-day. Tonight, however, he deferred to Kuminga and Ayo as the two turned defense into offense.

However, the real storyline is TJ Shannon, who continues to rise up the depth chart (much to my personal surprise) as another change-of-pace option in attacking the rim. His 15 points in 26 minutes comes after he looked to have found some confidence and comfort in his last few games.

There was a tweet from a few weeks ago that sticks with me about this team. It said the Wolves have on of the best 1-9s in the league, but have the most garbage 10- 15s as well.

Depth is so hard to really pull off, but the Wolves have the right kind of options. Instead of playing around waiting for a loophole, Minnesota just straight up outperformed and out delivered what the Grizzlies could do off the bench.

The Wolves will face off against Julius Randle and the Brooklyn Nets Monday evening, 6:30pm CT on Peacock, as the two former allies will fight over who deserves their low ppg elbow touches. Considering all of this is about raising the floor, the 3-12 Nets probably wish they pursued Gobert instead of Randle.

#2914. I never wanted the American Dream and I felt like something was wrong with me—until I moved abroad

I got my first job at 17, working the register at an Eckerd's drugstore in Texas. My manager was only a few years older than me, and while my coworkers seemed motivated by the idea of working their way up to his position, I felt no desire to follow that path.

It all felt suffocating: the added responsibility, the longer hours, the idea that this was the ladder I was supposed to want to climb. I wanted to work, but I couldn't wrap my head around building my whole identity around a job.

I never wanted the American Dream I saw so many people around me striving to achieve. For a long time, I wondered if something was wrong with me. It wasn't until I finally made a long-term move abroad about a decade ago that I realized it was okay to want something different.

After college, I got a job in travel sales. I was good at it. Talking about travel all day suited the wanderlust I'd carried since my first trip abroad in my teens.

I continued working in sales, marketing and the odd temp role for years. But they were always just jobs. I didn't feel like they defined me or feel a calling to an industry or title the way people around me seemed to.

Of course, I wanted financial stability, but I was unwilling to sacrifice my time and well-being to get it. I just didn't connect with the version of ambition that meant grinding toward a corner office. What I wanted the most wasn't status; it was freedom, growth and new experiences.

The wanderlust eventually caught up to me, and at 26 I moved to Spain to teach English. Even though I was working, people saw it as taking a year off from my career and warned me it could damage my resume. I simply didn't care. Living for my resume had never made sense to me.

Looking back, I think being abroad offered an escape from the pressure to chase the traditional path of the American Dream. There, I felt free to enjoy life and figure out who I was without worrying about what box I needed to tick next.

I wanted to stay in Spain, but I couldn't find a visa that would let me live there long-term, so I returned to the U.S. I became a serial job hopper for almost 10 years, quitting a job every year to spend summers visiting friends in the Netherlands, the U.K. and Switzerland or solo traveling in Spain on the money I'd saved up.

On the personal side, I wasn't in a rush to get married or have kids. In my 30s, single and childfree, I felt like I was failing at adulthood, even though deep down, I didn't actually want the things I was supposedly behind on. Settling down just because everyone else was doing it never felt right to me.

And I couldn't shake the dream of living in Europe. In my 20s, people mostly assumed my desire to travel was a phase. By my 30s, the conversations had shifted and I was getting pulled aside. Friends, family and colleagues all had some version of the same message: It was time to get serious and build a life in Texas.

But my gut said to try one more time. So I did.

In 2015, on my fourth attempt, I finally made it to Spain for good. It was scary to shake things up when everyone around me was settling down, but deep down I knew at 35, this was my chance to stop trying to conform and start building a life that felt right for me.

In my new home, the pace was slower. I quickly noticed that people actually took their vacations. Strangers at a dinner party didn't immediately ask me what I did for work. Adults in their 30s, 40s and beyond were out living full social lives. Single, childfree women my age didn't feel like an anomaly.

Living abroad didn't solve everything overnight. I still had to figure out how to support myself. But having the space to define myself professionally without constant judgment changed what felt possible.

In 2017, I launched She Hit Refresh, where I help women over 30 figure out how to move to Europe and build new lives for themselves. It's not something I could have created from anywhere else, with any other experience than my own.

In the process, I've met so many others who left corporate America behind to create something different for themselves. I've seen more women asking the same questions I spent years asking myself. Burnt out, disillusioned with hustle culture, realizing that checking all the boxes of the American Dream they grew up on didn't deliver the feeling they were promised.

We're all figuring it out together; each of us writing a version of a life that didn't fit a standard template. Moving abroad helped me understand that success, for me, was never about earning more or owning more. It was always about more ease, more time, more space, more life.

For years, I thought I was behind. I felt like something was wrong with me for wanting a different kind of life. Looking back, I just hadn't found my place yet.

Cepee Tabibian is the founder of She Hit Refresh, where she's helped over 24,000 women age 30+ plan their move abroad. Grab her free Move Abroad After 30 Guide to start planning your own move. The daughter of Colombian and Iranian immigrants, Cepee grew up in Houston, Texas, before becoming an immigrant herself in Spain. Follow her @shehitrefresh.

#2912. Mikal Bridges is finding his groove at the right time

Remember when we all said we wouldn't care about the regular season that much after the championship? That lasted long…

As the Knicks stumbled out to a 0-3 start that didn't seem to stabilize that much until this four-game winning streak that's brought them to 10-6 ahead of tonight's game against Indiana, fans didn't seem to have the patience they promised after this same group showed they can be trusted to step up when it counts.

At the center of that impatience was Mikal Bridges, who, despite putting up a second straight awesome playoff run and fully validating the trade by helping hoist a Larry O'Brien Trophy, was the common target early on as he struggled to find his groove.

Through 11 games, he was averaging just 5.9 points a game on a rough 32.9% from the field and 26.5% from deep. He'd be benched early in games where he didn't have it for Landry Shamet, he looked even more apprehensive than usual, and his defense wasn't as impactful as usual.

We've seen bad stretches from Bridges plenty over the last two years, but this was just maddening. The ring was supposed to free him; it was supposed to allow him to tap into the best of his game for extended stretches. The weight was off his shoulders.

Instead, it just seemed to get heavier? At times, it looked like the yips, basketball edition.

But this is why you exercise patience. These guys are champions; they know how to manage the highs and lows. When they look lost, they'll figure it out.

Body language is a big deal sometimes. You started to see him break out of his shell against the Pacers, where he looked for his shot early and often. When his jumper failed him, he got to the rim. He wasn't getting to the line, but he was attacking gaps and making good reads. The activity was back defensively.

He didn't seem to carry this over into Toronto a few days later, but a pep talk from Jose Alvarado might've done the trick:

Suddenly, he was everywhere defensively. A switch seemed to flip, and it looked like he was back in that Boston series. Needing a big late comeback, he set the tone defensively. Everything he did was purposeful, as he was a +15 in the overtime victory.

The offense came back around two nights later when the Knicks really needed it. Despite being -14 in 30 minutes, he put up an efficient 17 points.

The confidence to take shots like these just wasn't there a few weeks ago. He'd miss one or two threes and just stop shooting. He'd subconsciously pass up shots that had been a part of his game for years. It's pivotal that even when the ball isn't going down that a shooter keeps shooting, and we just didn't see that.

This article comes at a great time after he called game against the Blazers on Saturday night, but I think it shows that he's got his confidence back more than anything. Watch back the tape and see if you notice anything:

First, it's the fact that he consciously looks for the ball off the inbound. A big problem with the Knicks' final-possession offense is that they have one look and no backup plan. We saw that against Indiana with KAT and we see it all the time where they just screen for Jalen Brunson to get the ball and get out of the way.

Here, Bridges goes from a screener to releasing to pop up as a backup option. Then, look at this screenshot:

There are three whole seconds left, and Toumani Camara is on a hard closeout. There is nobody near Andre Drummond underneath the basket. If Bridges immediately hits him, it's an easy dunk (or is it, with how bad his finishing has been…)

In some ways, that's the correct read. A broken inbound has left the opponent scrambling and you have an easy, game-tying bucket under the basket. Maybe if he didn't make the shot, this would be discussed more.

Regardless, the confidence of Bridges matters a whole helluva lot more than winning a regular season game. This is a guy you need in April, May, and June, not November. A 26-point blown lead sucks, blowing the first 15+-point second half lead in the regular season since April 2023 sucks, but you want him taking this shot.

Maybe he just doesn't like the Blazers. That's two game-winners against them in his three years as a Knick.

Now, if only Josh Hart can figure it out too…

#2911. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2904. I’ve studied over 200 kids—to avoid raising a people pleaser, ditch 6 parenting habits: It starts in ‘ordinary, well-intentioned moments’

We all want to raise children who are kind, respectful and considerate. But sometimes, the very behaviors we praise are the ones teaching our children to put themselves last.

A child who never complains, always agrees and goes out of their way to make others happy might seem like a parent's dream. But what if they've simply learned that being easy to love means trying to please everyone all the time?

As a conscious parenting coach who's worked with over 200 families, I've seen how people pleasing can begin in the most ordinary, well-intentioned moments at home.

Here are six parenting habits worth reconsidering.

We love telling our children how easygoing they are, especially when they rarely complain or make demands. But when being easy earns our approval, children may start believing their needs are a burden and their feelings are better kept to themselves.

What to do instead: Make room for your child's disappointment, frustration and disagreement, even when it's inconvenient. They need to know they don't have to be happy or agreeable to be accepted by you.

When children question our decisions, it's tempting to respond with "Don't talk back" or "Because I said so." But when we repeatedly shut down their opinions, we may be teaching them that having a voice comes at the cost of a connection with us.

What to do instead: Separate disrespectful behavior from differences in perspective. You can say, "You don't have to agree with me. I want to understand what you think."

Statements that start with "After everything I've done for you…" may seem harmless in the moment, but they can leave children feeling responsible for their parents' emotional well-being.

When children hear this sentiment after misbehaving, they may become more concerned with how to make us feel better instead of thinking about what they did wrong. And they can begin confusing being good with keeping other people happy.

What to do instead: Address your child's behavior without making them feel guilty for disappointing you. Children need to learn to take responsibility for their actions, not for managing their parents' emotions.

We praise children for sharing, compromising and letting others have their way. But when self-sacrifice is consistently celebrated, children may begin to believe that being a good person means putting themselves last.

What to do instead: Teach your child that kindness doesn't require self-sacrifice. Let them know it's okay to opt out, express what they want or choose not to share something. Someone else's disappointment doesn't automatically mean they've done something wrong.

We teach children to say "please," "thank you" and "sorry," but how often do we teach them that they're allowed to say "no"?

When children are expected to hug relatives, share everything or agree in order to avoid offending someone, they may learn that other people's comfort matters more than their own.

What to do instead: Let your child know they can disagree with an adult, decline a hug or express a different opinion without being impolite or disrespectful. Respect means treating others with consideration, not automatically doing what they want.

Children notice when we say yes even though we're overwhelmed, apologize for things that aren't our fault or avoid difficult conversations because we don't want to upset anyone.

We can tell our children to stand up for themselves all day long, but if they watch us constantly abandon our own needs to keep the peace, they'll learn something very different.

What to do instead: Let your children see you set boundaries, express your needs and respectfully disappoint people when necessary. Show them that saying no doesn't make someone selfish and that healthy relationships can withstand disagreement.

Raising a confident child means teaching them that being kind and caring for others doesn't have to come at the expense of being themselves. Because the goal isn't to raise a child who's liked by everyone. It's to raise one who doesn't have to lose themselves to be liked.

Reem Raouda is a certified conscious parenting coach, speaker and author dedicated to one core idea: that loving your child and making them feel safe are not the same thing. She is the founder of The Safe Mom and creator of The Safe Mom Masterclass, helping parents raise emotionally healthy children through emotional safety, connection and self-awareness. Find her on Instagram.

#2902. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

#2900. U.K.-India trade deal faces a stiff test over Scotch whisky import taxes

Within months of taking effect, the India-U.K. trade deal is facing an early test, as New Delhi resists extending preferential tariffs to bulk Scotch whisky imports.

Duty on bulk Scotch whisky imports was expected to be slashed to 75% then gradually to 40% over 10 years as per the trade agreement. However, a Reuters report Thursday said that India now plans to levy a 162.5% duty on these imports, higher than the 150% in place before the trade deal.

While India's commerce ministry did not respond to CNBC's request for comments, experts said that authorities in India classify bulk Scotch as a raw material and claim the preferential tariffs accorded in the deal apply only to imported bottled Scotch.

Amid the confusion over tax rates, thousands of liters of bulk Scotch in more than 80 shipping containers belonging to Pernod, Diageo and Bacardi are stuck at Indian ports, according to Reuters.

"Ministers are urgently working with the Government of India to resolve this issue to ensure UK businesses can export under the tariffs agreed in the trade deal," a U.K. government spokesperson told CNBC.

Distillers in India use bulk Scotch in their own locally blended whisky brands. As a result, Indian tax authorities are classifying bulk Scotch as "compound alcoholic preparations" which do not qualify for the preferential rate, said Sohrab Bararia, partner and leader of global trade at Grant Thornton Bharat.

Meanwhile, distillers argue that "genuine Scotch whisky" does not cease to be whisky merely because it is imported in bulk containers or used later for blending and bottling in India, Bararia said.

After three years, including 14 rounds of rigorous negotiations, India and the U.K. finalized the deal last year, which is expected to boost bilateral trade by 25.5 billion pounds ($34 billion) per year by 2040.

Officials from Diageo and Chivas Brothers had cheered the trade deal and underscored its importance for Scotch and Scotland.

"India is the world's largest whisky market by volume, so Scotch was always going to be one of the biggest prizes for the UK in this deal," said Reema Bhattacharya, head of Asia research at risk intelligence company Verisk Maplecroft.

Instead of simply exporting bottled whisky, bulk scotch exports offer British producers long-term ties with companies in India, deepening their stake in the South Asian country's domestic alcoholic beverage industry, Bhattacharya said.

In the global spirits markets, while the U.S., Europe, and China are facing volume contraction, ageing populations, and shifting social habits, nearly 100 million new consumers will be of legal drinking age in India over the next five years, Indian brokerage HDFC Institutional Research said in a report in September.

The alcoholic beverage market in India is dominated by Indian-made foreign liquors (IMFL), as per the report. IMFL refers to alcoholic beverages produced in India by combining local spirits often with imported malts and flavorings.

Greater access to bulk Scotch stocks at competitive prices helps Indian distilleries to improve blending quality for premium whisky products while maintaining cost efficiency, experts said. However, the latest dispute poses a major threat to these operations.

The lack of preferential tariffs on bulk Scotch could have "substantial" financial implications for importers, Parth Shah, partner at Deloitte India, told CNBC. "This could particularly affect Indian manufacturers who have invested in domestic blending and bottling operations using imported bulk Scotch whisky," he added.

Diageo-owned United Spirits, Pernod Ricard and Indian firms Allied Blenders and Radico Khaitan own and market some of the popular whisky brands in India.

Companies and industry bodies, the International Spirits and Wine Association and Confederation of Indian Alcoholic Beverage Companies, did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

A spokesperson for one of the whisky makers told CNBC that industry bodies were in talks with the commerce ministry to "represent adherence to the India-UK FTA." The person did not wish to be identified as they fear it could lead to a potential backlash from regulators.

"The lesson for the EU and other partners negotiating with India is that securing a headline tariff reduction is only half the battle," Bhattacharya of Maplecroft Verisk said, adding that partners need to pay close attention to whether "concessions translate into actual market access."

#2899. Preview: Utah Jazz Aim to Tame Kyrie and the Mavericks

On the heels of a perplexing report that Utah Jazz management were considering Kyrie Irving as a potential trade target, the man himself lands in Salt Lake City for a Sunday night showdown between the Mavericks and the Jazz. The Jazz are coming off an impressive LA swing, where they dispatched both the Clippers and the Lakers, and a less impressive home loss to the Phoenix Suns in the NBA Cup. The Mavericks are on the second game of a back to back, after beating the Clippers at home by 10 on Saturday night.

It will be interesting to see which Josh Coach Hardy deploys to try to contain the young phenom. Strategic double teams helped frustrate Luka in LA and this may help get the ball out of Cooper's hands.

In their first meeting in Dallas on November 6th, it was PJ Washington who hurt the Jazz with a team high 26 points. Washington sat out last night's game against the Clippers, dealing with illness and a shoulder injury, and may not be available Sunday night. The Mavericks will also be without Biberovic, de Larrea, Lively and Lawal.

Utah will be without Jaren Jackson Jr., who is sidelined until mid to late December with an MCL tear, and Lauri Markkanen, who will be nursing a fractured hand for another 3-4 weeks. The Jusuf Nurkic news is better: he has been cleared for on court activities and will be re-evaluated next week. Jaxson Hayes, who was almost perfect from the floor Friday night, is expected to play after taking a nasty fall in the fourth quarter against the Suns.

#2896. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guidance; Extension of Comment Period

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guide for Traditional Community Banking Organizations

Agencies seek comment on proposed third-party risk management guidance and issue statement on community bank engagement with core service providers

#2895. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2893. Warren Buffett quadruples Thanksgiving gifts: A breakdown of how much wealth he's giving away

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Warren Buffett, 96, more than quadrupled his Thanksgiving donations to four family foundations this year to $5.5 billion.

The gifts consist of Berkshire Class B shares that have been converted from his roughly $160 billion of Class A shares.

The increase is in line with his new goal, announced last July, to give away all of his Berkshire Hathaway shares "within about eight years," with no additional annual donations going to the Gates Foundation.

Around $2.2 billion of shares goes to the Susan Thompson Buffett Foundation, named for his late first wife, with the three foundations run by his children, Susie Buffett's Sherwood Foundation, the Howard G. Buffett Foundation, and Peter Buffett's NoVo Foundation each getting $1.1 billion each.

In July of last year, Buffett gave shares worth $4.6 billion to the Gates Foundation with another $1.4 billion going to the family foundations.

This July, the family foundations received $5.9 billion in stock, bringing the year's total to $11.4 billion, an increase of almost 55% from last year, even as the Gates Foundation received nothing.

In comments included in the Berkshire news release this week announcing the new gifts, Buffett reaffirmed his confidence in his children's ability to disburse his fortune over time, saying they "have given thoughtfully to worthy causes for decades, and I trust 100% both their abilities and their fidelity."

He also said his confidence in Berkshire is "as strong as ever."

Recalling that in last year's lengthier Thanksgiving message he had said, "Kindness is costless but also priceless," Buffett wrote, " A year later, I would only add that it compounds, and at a better rate than anything Charlie [Munger] and I ever bought."

"Both of us received beyond measurement costless benefits from a few teachers such as Ben Graham, Joe Rosenfield and Tom Murphy. Because of this we not only prospered mightily but were able to pass along many of the benefits we received.

"Much of my good fortune has come in the form of people who were kind to me and expected nothing in return. I'm grateful to every one of them.

"This Thanksgiving, thank one of those selfless people in your own life. Better yet, be a benefactor for someone else."

Berkshire Hathaway's fourth quarter rally that had produced a string of record highs cooled this week as both classes of its stock posted five straight daily declines.

Last Friday, Berkshire was up 19.1% since October 1. Now that gain has been cut to 14.5%

The S&P's decline of 8.6% this week, however, was even bigger than Berkshire's 5-day drop, as investors sold to lock in profits from AI-related stocks.

The benchmark index, along with the Dow Industrials, are in a correction after falling 10% from their recent highs, and the Nasdaq Composite has erased all of its year-to-date gains.

Berkshire's smaller decline increased its year-to-date outperformance of the S&P to 12.5 percentage points.

One Wall Street firm, however, thinks Berkshire's run-up has run its course.

Argus Research downgraded the B shares to hold from buy Friday, saying the stock is "fully valued" at about 25 times next year's estimate for operating earnings per share.

As foreshadowed by its 10-Q financial report released almost two weeks ago, Berkshire Hathaway's Q3 13F portfolio snapshot filed with the SEC on Monday showed it wasn't doing much buying and selling of equities this summer.

And the two largest additions, Lennar and Bending Spoons, had been previously disclosed, along with additional purchases at the start of the fourth quarter.

The most notable change we didn't expect was a 12% increase in Berkshire's Delta Air Lines position, bringing it just short of a 10% stake, now valued at $5.2 billion.

Exceeding 10% triggers a number of regulatory burdens, including the requirement to promptly disclose new buying or selling, so we may not see additional purchases going forward.

Delta's return to the portfolio after Warren Buffett sold all of Berkshire's airline stocks in 2020 has been something of a surprise given his bumpy ride with the industry over the years.

As expected, Berkshire continued to trim its Bank of America stake. After nine consecutive quarters of sales, it's been cut by 56% but remains the fifth largest portfolio holding at $21.4 billion.

Berkshire also cut another financial, Capital One, by almost 62%, although it was already a relatively small position, now worth $207 million.

Also notable are two positions that didn't change.

After adding $17 billion in Q2 to the Alphabet bet that had only been initiated last summer, Berkshire defied some expectations by keeping the holding unchanged.

Its 106 million shares are valued at $28.1 billion.

And for the fourth consecutive quarter, Berkshire's largest equity holding, Apple, didn't change. With a big boost from the stock's year-to-date gain of 26.1%, the position is now valued at more than $78 billion.

Warren Buffett loves to eat what many would call junk food and drink a lot of sugary beverages. A quarter-century ago, when he was 70, Buffett explained why he still thought a life insurance company would rate his life expectancy "considerably better than the average."

AUDIENCE MEMBER: Every time I hear what you like to eat, Warren, it makes me wonder what your cholesterol level is — (laughter) — or if you even worry about it...

WARREN BUFFETT: I do know the number, and I don't remember it. My doctor tells me, "It's a little high," but if he says it's a little high, it means it isn't that high, or he would — because he always tries to push me into making a few changes in my life.

But he — I've got a wonderful doctor. And I was lucky last year, because I hadn't been in to see him for about five years. And — (laughter) — due to — those guys cost a lot of money, I mean. (Laughter)

And due to purely an accident, a reaction to some other medicine I was given when I was out of the city, he got a hold of me, and then he shamed me into having a physical. And it was extremely lucky, because I had a polyp in the colon that would have probably caused trouble, you know, within a couple of years.

I would say that if you ask my doctor, he would want me to make a few changes, but he would also say that my life expectancy is probably a lot better than the average person of 70.

You know, I have no stress whatsoever. Zero. You know, I mean, I get to do what I love to do every day. You know, and I'm surrounded by people that are terrific.

So that problem in life just doesn't exist for me. You know, and I don't smoke or drink or, well, we'll end it right there. (Laughter)

And so, you know, if you were an underwriter for a life company, you would rate me considerably better than the average. You'd rate Charlie better than average, too.

And I'm sure that, you know, I could change it slightly, perhaps, on the probabilities, you know, if I change my diet dramatically or something. But it's very unlikely to happen.

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,210,976,168,580

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

#2888. The Wizards have a chance to advance in the NBA Cup

The Washington Wizards won against the Chicago Bulls last Friday. This wasn't just another regular season win. It's also their second win in NBA Cup group play, a franchise record (I'll go over why in a bit). Washington also improves to 2-1 in East Group C play.

Washington has one more game for the NBA Cup which is against the Boston Celtics on Tuesday. If they win, Washington improves to 3-1 but are mathematically eliminated from winning East Group C. It is possible that the Wizards could make the knockout stage as a wild card team if the Celtics win on Friday against the Atlanta Hawks. This would give the Celtics, Hawks and Wizards 3-1 records with the Celtics winning the group.

Now how do the Wizards claim a wildcard spot as a second place team? That's through cumulative point differential in regulation. Currently, the Wizards are third in the wildcard standings behind the Cleveland Cavaliers (+21) and the Hawks (+13) who have the same point differential as the Wizards but the Hawk hold the tiebreaker.

It will be a sign of progress if Washington can make the knockout stage of the NBA Cup. This is because Washington has finished in last place every year since 2023-24 when this tournament was introduced. In the first two years of this tournament, the Wizards finished with 0-4 group play records, while they went 1-3 last season. The Wizards also set a record two wins in group play this season, which is encouraging.

Hopefully, luck will be on their side this week when they play the Celtics on Tuesday.

(And yes, I know they host the Orlando Magic tonight).

#2885. Knicks Bulletin: ‘I like the city of Portland’

If you are like me, you thought you could go to bed early yesterday.

If you are like me, then you realize you were not doing so, because at the end of the day it's the Knicks and they do (yes, after the championship too) Knicks things.

Oh, well, good for all, we stayed awake, and tears rained at the end of the day.

On Mikal Bridges maintaining his composure through a tough start to the season:

"He's the same. Whether he's quote-unquote struggling, he just keeps playing and he doesn't let anything impact him. He's gonna let it fly, and if he can push it, he's gonna push it. He's gonna try to defend, but he's not gonna get too high. He's not gonna get too low, and he's been there and done that. So for him to have the ball in his hands at that point in the game, there's a lot of confidence not only coming from him, but from his teammates and the staff."

On managing the rotation without Karl-Anthony Towns and OG Anunoby:

"I have an idea. I always try to go into the game with a game plan with my minutes sheet. Very seldom do I follow it. We'll have a minutes sheet going into the game."

On Landry Shamet's effort, shooting and defensive versatility:

"I felt he was a really good basketball player coming in. The biggest thing is he plays hard. So even when he's not shooting — because he hasn't shot the ball great every single game — but when he's not shooting the ball, you still feel his impact.

"He plays so hard. He's probably asked to come out of games more than anybody else because he really gasses himself being out there. Every play is that meaningful to him.

"Obviously, you can't leave him open. He's been knocking down shots for the most part. We put him on the ball in pick-and-roll situations, guarding pick-and-roll guys; we put him off the ball so he's had to chase guys. We've even put him on bigger wings and he's done a heck of a job fighting those wings. He's been phenomenal for us. We hope he continues to be that way."

On Shamet exceeding his expectations and being capable of starting for winning teams:

"It's a good question. I didn't know if he'd be this], because I think he can start for a lot of teams in this league, and I'm talking winning teams, not just any old team, but for winning teams.

"I didn't necessarily look at him that way, but I did feel he was definitely a rotational player for this team, because again, you can't teach some of the things that he does out on the floor. They're special. And it's hard to teach that, but in the same breath, you need it in order to have some success, in my opinion."

On James Wiseman's performance against Portland:

"I thought James was great. He ran the floor in both directions, he talked defensively, he impacted the ball at the rim in a phenomenal way. I thought he was really good in his minutes."

On the importance of quick decisions and purposeful movement in the Knicks' offense:

"We have to make sure that every time we cut, it's a scoring cut and our decisions are quick."

On Shamet's off-ball movement creating opportunities for teammates:

"You can tell when a guy goes from Point A to Point B with a little bit of velocity. A guy like Landry Shamet, he is doing it every single time. With him shooting the ball the way he's shooting it, that gravity that he carries when he moves, you can't really see it or feel it if you don't know the game well. But when he moves that quickly from Point A to Point B, everybody is just leaning just a little bit in that direction, which may create an opening or an advantage for your teammate that's away from him."

On getting every Knicks player to move aggressively without the ball:

"Trying to get everybody to do that all the time is huge. Because the defense doesn't have a chance to catch up, they don't have a chance to get set, and they just gotta react. And sometimes they'll overreact because you're going so hard. And you can see it as the game goes along. If guys aren't doing it, we try to mention it to him."

On Deni Avdija's ability to draw fouls and influence officials:

"For a young guy, he's one of the best in the NBA when it comes to gamesmanship with the officials. He knows how to drive, and as soon as he feels the slightest hit, he can react good enough, like, I'd call a foul for him. He can react good enough to get the officials' attention and get him to the free-throw line."

On Avdija's development as a shooter and offensive threat:

"He's figured it out at a really young age. He's also worked extremely hard on his 3-point game, so you can't give him space. You gotta play up on him, and now he's strong enough and quick enough to go through you and get by you and finish and/or get fouled. He's a handful."

On intentionally pairing Jose Alvarado with Jalen Brunson:

"A lot of times we'll pair Jose and Jalen together and we went into that consciously thinking about doing that against Minnesota, and tonight we'll go into the game consciously thinking about it so all three of those guys will play."

On discovering the Alvarado-Brunson combination against Toronto:

"Against Toronto in the second game, we threw Jose out there with Jalen and we liked it but that was not on my minutes sheet. Going into our last game … he was actually on the minutes sheet to play with Jalen some, and I kinda liked that combination."

On Tyler Kolek remaining part of the guard rotation:

"Tyler's been playing well so Tyler will play some without Jose and/or Jalen on the floor."

On rarely following any predetermined rotation plan:

"I go in with a minutes sheet. I don't know if we've followed the minutes sheet once so far this year or let alone even last year."

On allowing players' families to travel with the team to build trust:

"In order to get players' trust and get true connectivity, it's anybody associated with them. That's why it's important to have families around."

On Mikal Bridges overcoming his shooting struggles through consistent work:

"Yeah, that's just who he is. I don't know how many times I've said this before, but when you continue to put work in your game every single day, and things may not be falling in your favor, but you keep doing the right thing every single day, you keep chipping away, chipping away. Eventually, things are gonna turn for you. And over the past week or two, things have been turning for him, and now he's on a roll."

On repeatedly hitting game-winning shots against Portland:

"I like the city of Portland. [Damian Lillard] is one of my best friends. I like the city of Portland."

On maintaining his effort and making plays in crunch time:

"I try to play hard throughout the whole game, and obviously when the time gets short and it's time to win a game, I think everybody's senses are heightened. Just try to win a game and make a play."

"Yeah, just trying to get Jalen open and create space. How Josh threw it kind of indicated I was going to be open, so just go make a play. It's great. First, you win the game, but second, seeing the joy in those guys. Obviously, tonight with the alumni stuff, it's a pretty cool moment to be in that situation, especially after a tough fourth quarter. So it felt good."

On sharing Bridges' struggles while adjusting to the season:

"Me and him are in the same boat… we're trying to figure it out."

On Bridges remaining prepared despite his early-season struggles:

"Knowing that any good and bad stretch in the NBA can change very quickly, he's always in a position to put himself in a good situation."

On Bridges' game-winner rescuing the Knicks after their fourth-quarter collapse and his dumb late foul:

"Shoutout to Mikal for making the big-time 3, to kind of mask the nonsense that happened."

On the Karl-Anthony Towns unfair criticism during his years in Minnesota:

"When we first got him in Minnesota, the playoffs, they were saying how he's a very good player that couldn't win, couldn't get the team to the playoffs. Then, when we got to the playoffs, any time we struggled, it was him that took the fall, unfairly. And I think it was great to see not only the impact that he had on this championship team, but to see the way that he's playing now. … I love KAT; I was happy for him to get to the mountaintop; I was happy for him to play a major role.

"Then, when we made a deep run, they said you can't win a title with him, and he got the blame."

#2883. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2882. Bears vs. Lions odds: Opening lines for Week 12 matchup

The traditional Thanksgiving games in Detroit and Dallas don't always feature high stakes, but this Thursday's Noon kickoff in Motown between the Chicago Bears and the Detroit Lions will see the winner move into first place in the NFC North. Both teams won today to move to 8-2 to set up the critical NFC matchup, and with the Minnesota Vikings loss tonight, the winner on Thanksgiving will end the week as the two-seed in the NFC.

Our partners at FanDuel Sportsbook list the Bears as a road favorite (-2.5), with the Chicago moneyline at -132, and the point total over/under at 55.5.

The Bears haven't beaten the Lions since 2023, and they haven't won in Detroit since 2021, which was also the last time they won a Thanksgiving game.

In their two games a season ago, the Lions won big at Ford Field 52 to 21 in Week 2, then won on a last-second field goal in the season finale 19 to 16 at Soldier Field. So Bears head coach Ben Johnson is still looking for his first win against his former team, and you know that will be a big part of the narrative leading up to the game.

The Bears didn't cover today's 11.5-point spread against the New Orleans Saints, but they jumped out to a 16-0 lead, then held on and coasted to a 21 to 13 win. The Bears have won five in a row, and they never trailed once in any of those games.

The Lions are on a hot streak of their own, winning their sixth straight today, 31 to 21 against the Tampa Bay Buccaneers. Running back Jahmyr Gibbs is having an outstanding year for the Lions and is among the leading candidates for the NFL Offensive Player of the Year Award, but he's just one of several weapons Chicago's defense will need to contend with.

What are your early thoughts about the game and the Bears opening as the favorite?

#2881. State and federal battle over prediction markets regulation heats up after midterms

The turf war between states and the federal government is intensifying after Democrats secured control of Congress and key governorships on midterm elections night.

While it was widely expected Democrats would step up oversight of the emerging, multibillion-dollar industry if they won control of Congress, the actions after the elections have come from primarily Republican-led states.

At the same time, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission — the federal regulator for prediction markets —  has ramped up its oversight of "mention" markets, where traders speculate on what individuals will say during a speech or broadcast. It's a type of event contract the agency has said is susceptible to manipulation.

CNBC first reported on Tuesday that the CFTC placed a hold on the certification of markets related to President Donald Trump's words in mid-November. Kalshi said it was complying with the order, but CEO Tarek Mansour said at an Axios event on Tuesday that the company disagreed with the regulatory decision.

On Thursday, the Senate passed a bipartisan bill that would ban CFTC-registered entities from listing contracts involving terrorism, assassination, war or military operations. The measure also bars contracts that are "reasonably expected" to resolve based on an individual's death.

Utah Attorney General Derek Brown filed 14 criminal charges against Kalshi and its founders, Mansour and Luana Lopes Lara, on Oct. 28, asserting the company violated a state law that prohibits gambling.

Kalshi's founders are expected to appear in person at Utah's Third District Court on Dec. 4, according to court documents.

"This is a crazy overreach; Utah is clearly playing politics. We look forward to putting a stop to this in court, just like we did in Arizona," said Kalshi's spokesperson Dani Lever.

In a separate, civil complaint on Oct. 20, Utah also named institutional market maker Susquehanna International Group as a defendant, along with Kalshi.

Though Arizona Attorney General Kris Mayes filed a criminal complaint against Kalshi in March, a federal judge blocked the case from proceeding in April.

Louisiana's Republican attorney general also filed a complaint to ban multiple exchanges from operating in the state on Nov. 12. A state judge ordered a temporary restraining order against the prediction market companies that same day.

"Who would have thought that these red states, that were reluctant to jump into the litigation arena, ended up having the most boldest and most innovative solutions," said gambling attorney Daniel Wallach, noting that Louisiana and Utah's latest lawsuits were led by Republican attorneys general.

In response, the CFTC sued Louisiana on Nov. 13. Separately, the federal agency filed suit against Arkansas after that state pursued civil enforcement against Kalshi.

The CFTC has sued 12 states as it tries to defend what it views as its exclusive jurisdiction to regulate prediction markets.

Meanwhile, the CFTC may soon add commissioners. President Donald Trump sent bipartisan nominations to the U.S. Senate on Nov. 12 to fill two of the four open seats on the commission. Chairman Michael Selig has been the only member since his nomination was approved by the Senate in December 2025.

Nathan Anonick, a Republican, and Matthew Rowland, a Democrat, appeared before the Senate Agriculture Committee on Tuesday. Multiple senators questioned them on the issue of prediction markets. Both demurred when asked whether they viewed sports-related event contracts as gambling.

The CFTC's position faced another setback in a complicated decision by the Fourth U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals this week. The court ruled 2-1 that Maryland can enforce its state gambling law against Kalshi, though the judges disagreed on the reasoning.

"Judges can't agree with each other on why we're wrong," Kalshi spokesperson Elisabeth Diana said in a statement about the decision. "This splintered decision, including three separate contradictory opinions, demonstrates there is no unified coherent legal argument against prediction markets."

Overall, prediction markets have now seen three losses and one win at the circuit court level. It's widely expected the question of whether states or the federal government can regulate sports-related event contracts will be decided by the Supreme Court.

Kalshi on Nov. 12  ended its advisory relationship with Donald Trump Jr. The prediction platform pointed to his venture capital firm 1789 Capital having a stake in rival platform Polymarket.

Though 1789 Capital announced its holding in Polymarket in August, Kalshi and the president's son ended their relationship after Democrats secured a majority in the House and Senate. Democratic lawmakers are expected to probe companies with relationships to the Trump family.

Nine Democratic senators, led by Adam Schiff, D-California, on Monday sent a letter to the CFTC asking it to reverse a directive to platforms not to geofence their offerings when they receive unfavorable court rulings stopping exchanges from operating in certain states.

Geofencing involves the use of location technology to block users in certain jurisdictions from participating in events contracts.

"Your unilateral decision directly conflicts with active court orders, undermines state and tribal law, and must be reversed," the senators wrote.

Meanwhile, Democratic Rep. Ro Khanna, D-California, has called for Congress to ban sports-related prediction markets entirely.

"Kalshi, a $40B company, lets 18 year olds bet on sports. They call it a 'prediction market.' It's gambling," he wrote in a post on X on Wednesday. "Investigate and ban sports prediction markets."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2880. Federal Reserve announces College Fed Challenge winners

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The University of California, Los Angeles won the 23rd Annual National College Fed Challenge.

The University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) won the 23rd annual national College Fed Challenge on Friday, a team competition that encourages undergraduate students to learn about the U.S. economy, monetary policymaking, and the role of the Federal Reserve System. Teams analyze economic and financial conditions and formulate a monetary policy recommendation, modeling the Federal Open Market Committee. The team, from Los Angeles, California, represented the San Francisco District and included Aarushi Maheshwari, Annika Chan, Atul Prasanth, Caitlyn Cox, Finn Karish, Eli Brooks (alternate), Kennedy Yang (alternate), Manasi Vartak (alternate), Ronojoy Borpujari (alternate), and Sahil Sudani (alternate). The team's advisers were Chris Surro and Ali Haider Ismail.

"I am very happy to congratulate the students who competed in this year's College Fed Challenge, especially the winners and finalists," said Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh. "They took on the same big questions we policymakers face. I'm impressed by their talent, their rigor, and their eagerness to think hard about monetary policy and the economy."

This year's College Fed Challenge included 141 schools from 34 states across the nation, the most in the competition's history. Teams submitted video presentations or participated in local competitions last month. Twenty-one semi-finalist teams then participated in question-and-answer sessions held earlier this month, and seven finalists were invited to the Federal Reserve Board for the national finals. The other national finalists were the University of Chicago in second place and the University of Maryland, College Park in third place. Teams with honorable mentions include Cleveland State University, Harvard College, Pennsylvania State University, and St. Lawrence University.

Teams were evaluated on their economic analysis, responses to the judges' questions, teamwork, and presentations.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

20th Street and Constitution Avenue N.W., Washington, DC 20551
