#2879. Inside the Suns: Ryan Dunn, Koa Peat, a wake up call?

Welcome to Inside the Suns, a deep down analysis of the current Phoenix Suns team in which the Fantable — a round table of Bright Siders — give their takes on the Suns' latest issues and news.

GuarGuar: I've been really impressed with Ryan so far this season. He's showing more control and poise in his game. He's not recklessly driving at 100 mph to the rim every time he touches the ball. The jump shot mechanics look better, and he's been impressive as a roll man this year. He's been a pleasant surprise.

zenzino: I love it! How could I not? As I wrote in a previous Fantable (or thread, I can't remember), Dunn was considered by many (including me) to be the odd man out for rotational minutes this season. However, he became the poster child for the fact that progress is not linear. After a sophomore year where the improvement was slight (especially alongside Oso's improvement), Dunn has made a leap in his third year.

We never questioned his defense. He reportedly put in a lot of work on his shot this off-season. As he said himself, he 'has to trust in the work'. This becomes evident during a game. When he has time to think about it, he'll either pass up a shot or look more like last year's shooting. When he doesn't have time to think, his shot looks really good and goes in more often than not.

The more he works, and the less time he has to think, the shots will go down, and he'll gain enough confidence to think and shoot.

This is also a lesson not to give up on Sheer. He may have a sophomore slump this season, but that doesn't mean he's not working and could come out later in the season or his third year to make a Dunn-like leap.

Ashton: I am a little curious why Dunn's minutes seem to vary by game. He is clearly the next man up at the power forward position. But, thus far, I have not paid enough attention to Dunn on the Fantable and he is impressing me as a future breakout candidate to start. So, my bad on not giving Ryan Dunn more love early.

Also, Brooks said he would be willing to play bench minutes? Jaw hits the floor as he is coming off one of his best games against the Sacramento Kings. He stated that is to work on his playmaking with the better spacing that the bench provides. I applaud his heroic statement, but I am calling BS. The real reason is so he can terrorize opposing bench unit defenses into therapy with his constant chirping. Still, an intriguing thought to consider. Does Ott buy-in? I doubt it.

That would make Ryan Dunn the starter for minutes if that were to come true. Some of the Suns' lineups would be rife for consideration in the comment sections.

OldAz: Personally, I love what Ryan has done this year. He has taken advantage of the lowered expectations, especially compared to last season when he was expected to come in and capture a larger role only to struggle with the new system and get stuck behind a number of veteran guards.

This season, most had written him off coming into the season and were fine throwing him into whatever trade would accomplish moving whatever core player that fan wanted to move. Instead, he ratcheted up his defensive intensity and the speed at which he plays and has made a significant impact.  My only complaint is with the coaching staff that seems to have way too long of a leash for veteran players to play emotionless, effortless basketball and only give a player like Ryan a few minutes despite their obvious positive impact on the overall result on the floor.

Ryan is not going to fill up the stat sheet, but he is going to hustle on defense, compete for rebounds, and is willing to shoot or drive when he is left open.  He is a perfect complement to Booker or any of the other "star" players on the court when Ott chooses to play him.  He is one of those players (along with Goodwin, Gillespie, and Kennard) that you can pair with either center and 2 of the core starters and get a pretty decent lineup because of their hustle and energy. The only difference is he is the only one of those 4 that also brings some length to the court.

I'm not overly worried about the recent little injuries, as I believe he has figured out that his energy and effort mean more than the stats he puts up each night. Hopefully Ott is starting to figure out the same thing.

Rod: I hated to see him get hurt in the Jazz game. Hopefully. he won't be out long because he's become an important part of the Suns' rotation. His offensive impact hasn't been great, but he's been playing more under control on that end and making positive contributions, both inside (5th most dunks on the team in just the 10th most minutes per game) and outside (42.3% from three). His biggest areas of impact have been on defense and in rebounding (per 36, he's tied with Bridges for 2nd at 8.6).

I'll admit that I was one of the people who was almost ready to give up on Ryan this season if he didn't show some improvement. Happily, he's come through for us and is playing very well and under control.

GuarGuar: I really hope it was a wake-up call. This starting lineup has been awful together, and the metrics don't lie. The Jalen Green PG experiment has been an abject failure, and the sooner we move off of it, the better we will be. The bench plays together as a unit and with a ton of energy, the complete opposite of our starting unit.

zenzino: I guess? How many wake-up calls do they need?

They supposedly had one in the first game of the season. The first game!

They definitely looked better against Denver and really came out against the Kings with aggressiveness, energy, and surprisingly good ball movement. It was the Kings, but maybe playing a few easier games in a row (Jazz & Pels next) will allow them to get some confidence/momentum.

Recent reports amidst all the trade rumors/ideas say that the Suns are not shopping Green. They like the roster they have and are content to give these guys time to gel. I don't know if it has anything to do with the benching, but we're definitely seeing some improvement.

It occurred to me the other day that it is the fans' job to (over)react from game to game and the coaches/FO's job NOT to react from game to game.

Ashton: I hate this question. Beat LA, Beat LA!

The fact of the matter is that the Lakers are that good. It was a nice salve to the wounds to see the Suns beat a good Nuggets team the day after. Which is exactly what the Suns need: beating the teams they are supposed to beat, but picking up a few wins against the teams that are not (Houston and Denver). The Lakers were not on the bingo card in the W category.

Still, Ott probably wisely threw in the towel against a very good Lakers squad and allowed his starters to watch real basketball from the sidelines without getting injured. I was fine with it from a strategic point of view, but I am not fine with it from a fan point of view.

OldAz: Maybe. It certainly couldn't have hurt as we have seen a better version of Brooks and Green since then, with more focus on the defensive end and moving the ball on offense instead of always forcing their own shots. I think Booker and Bridges were already playing well, but it really requires all the starters to be on the same page for that group to succeed.  The better play and effort from all has also allowed Ott to play a little more randomly with lineups.

Now the question is if the recent successes are more about this improved play and more diverse lineup combinations, or just a product of playing lesser teams that the Suns should handle like the Kings and Jazz.

Rod: You could call it that, or you could call it a message from Ott, "Play with some heart or you'll sit and watch your teammates show you what that means." Whatever you want to call it, it seems to have worked in the games since then.

You can have all the talent in the world, but without effort, players are just wasting the fans' time (and patience) when they're just going through the motions. I don't want to see the starters playing like that again, and hopefully they got that message from Ott loud and clear…and won't forget it.

GuarGuar: Koa is probably only useful as a small-ball 5 right now until he develops a jump shot, which looks to be a ways away. He has good instincts and can finish through contact pretty well, but he's not close to being ready for a consistent rotation spot in my opinion. Next year will probably be a different discussion.

zenzino: I think it's pretty telling that he has spent zero time with the Valley Suns. My wild speculation is that Ott wants him with the team and ready to play.

After looking great in quality minutes against the Lakers, Ott teased us with a 2nd-quarter appearance of Peat against the Nuggets. That lasted a minute. Literally. The next time we saw him was garbage time against the hapless Kings.

When this question was first sent to us at the beginning of the week, my answer was that Koa should definitely get some Brooks minutes. Or even some Bridges minutes. But mostly Brooks.

Up through the Lakers game, Brooks has been a shell of what we loved about him last season. Maybe, like cutting Sampson's hair (and Dillon's hair), Dillon's effort to not get techs this season has stripped him of his strength.

He was better against the Nuggets (got a tech) and was on fire against the Kings (no tech). Hopefully, he has turned a corner and will continue with the aggressive Villain we know and love. Techs and all.

Bridges is showing progress with every game, and we are beginning to see what the FO envisioned with this addition. 14 games is not even close to enough games for a starter to fully integrate. As I said, the FO has to take the long view and not overreact game to game.

So, barring injuries or a slide by either Brooks or Bridges, we will probably only see Koa in very specific matchup situations or garbage time. That's ok for now. There's a lot to learn from just being with the team. Especially if they continue the winning trend.

Ashton: How to answer this one? Peat's best game came against the Lakers. At least the Arizona guy gets it. But in the end, stats do not lie on small sample sizes. I am stealing a copy and paste from Hoops Nightly:

Peat has appeared in 8 games and averages 4.5 minutes, 3.1 points, 0.9 rebounds and 0.1 assists. He is shooting 64.7 percent from the field and 100.0 percent at the line, with a 0.0 mark from three. His true shooting is 68.2 percent and his usage is 21.1. The plus-minus is 3.8. These numbers come from very few minutes, so they say little yet. His minutes have ranged from 1.3 to 8.2 in a night, and he has not scored in three of the eight games.

Okay, with that out of the way (I hate talking stats – leave that to the board members), the stats still suggest that Peat's minutes should increase if only to increase the sample size. And consistent minutes would probably help rather than 8 minutes here and a throw-in one minute there.

Since the question asked if he "looks" ready for more rotational minutes, I would state an emphatic yes until proven otherwise.

OldAz: In small doses, certainly.  Just like we saw in the Jazz game, he needs to learn to play at this level without fouling, but that isn't going to happen at the G League level or during garbage minutes when the refs also just want to get the game over.

I think he should get about the same 5-6 minutes Dunn was getting earlier in the season, and given an opportunity to play more as he does well. To me, these minutes should be coming from Brooks, Green, Bridges, and even Booker, as up until the last few games the bench minutes have been better anyway, so Ott might as well mix and match more minutes with just 2 of those core 4 on the floor with a center and 2 bench players. Go ahead and compress the starters' minutes while this is working, save more strain on the starters' legs until later in the season and see who actually develops.

I know this will never happen, but the Suns really have to come up with a better way to give minutes to young players at the NBA level and start developing them faster than they have been.

Rod: Peat does look ready for more playing time, but for now, I think most of that time will come when Ott decides to play a small-ball lineup, so I don't think he'll be taking minutes away from anyone specifically. I've seen Ott try various players out in that role, and Koa just seems to be the best fit in that role. And considering the guy's built like a linebacker, I'm not at all certain that we should even call it "small" ball when he's at the 5.

The three quick fouls he got in the Jazz game don't change my mind. He may not be ready for a full-blown rotation spot, but he's ready for spot rotation minutes.

As always, many thanks to our Fantable members for all their extra effort this week!

"You can see his (Jalen Green's) speed. Anytime he's in open court, anytime he gets downhill, that's what we need. We need paint pressure. We're a predominantly jump-shooting team. Him and Miles (Bridges) provide some of that paint pressure, that downhill thrust that we need offensively." – Jordan Ott

"We're just gonna continue to talk about just stacking our small segments of time and that will build continually over time. We'll get better. We believe in that. We believe every time we're together, whether that's a meal, whether that's a shootaround, whether that's a film session, that we have to go all in, all in, all the time, and they'll continually stack." – Jordan Ott

"Collectively, we were in it for the full 48." – Jordan Ott

"I see Man-Man working every day. He's one of the hardest workers I've ever played with." – Miles Bridges

"We have guys that want to win, that want to play the right way." – Luke Kennard

On November 23, 1972, Charlie Scott recorded the Phoenix Suns' first-ever triple-double with 42 points, 10 rebounds, and 15 assists in a 128-122 victory over the Detroit Pistons on Thanksgiving Day.

On November 23, 1994, Wesley Person hit for 19 points to lead 10 double-figure scorers in Phoenix's 140-109 home win over the LA Clippers. The Suns became the third team in NBA history to have 10 different players score in double figures in the same game. No Suns player shot less than 50% from the field that day, and the team FG percentage was 64.7% while also hitting 47.1% of their three-point attempts.

On November 23, 2015, Devin Booker got his first career start for the Phoenix Suns, filling in for an injured Eric Bledsoe. Booker had 4 points, 1 assist, 4 rebounds, and 1 steal in his debut in the Suns' starting lineup as Phoenix fell to the San Antonio Spurs 98-84.

On November 24, 1989, the Suns reserves were outscored 16-0 to start the 4th quarter, so "only" up 28 points, head coach Cotton Fitzsimmons brought back his starters, allowing Jeff Hornacek to record the first triple-double of his career (22pts, 12 rebs, 15assists) in a 121-94 win over the Orlando Magic.

On November 25, 1995, down 110-100 with 90 seconds left, the Suns nailed 4 three-pointers and, after Nick Van Exel inexplicably allowed himself to be tied up with 6.7 seconds left, Michael Finley hit a buzzer-beater to defeat the LA Lakers, 114-113.

On November 27, 1977, Paul Westphal set a new franchise and career-high when he scored 48 points in a 115-97 win over the Denver Nuggets. Westphal was on fire all night, shooting 20-30 from the field and 8-10 FTs. When John MacLeod pulled him with 3:17 left in the game, fans began to chant, "We want Westy."

#2878. NFL says Parker Washington didn't control ball until he was out of bounds

Sunday's game between the Jaguars and Giants included a controversial decision in the fourth quarter on a play that looked like a touchdown catch by Jacksonville receiver Parker Washington.

The ruling on the field was that the catch was incomplete. Jaguars coach Liam Coen challenged the decision.

Replay review confirmed the ruling on the field, even though CBS rules analyst Gene Steratore said during the broadcast that he believed it was a catch.

After the game, pool reporter Pat Leonard spoke with NFL V.P. of instant replay Mark Butterworth.

"The ruling on the field was an incomplete pass," Butterworth said. "First, to overturn a call, we need clear and obvious video evidence to overturn the on-field ruling. It was determined that the receiver did not gain full control of the ball until he was out of bounds."

Butterworth confirmed that he was referring to the fact that, when Washington hit the ground, the ball was moving in Washington's hands.

Still, movement of the ball is permitted. Washington objectively remained in control of the ball.

And here's the real point. Movement doesn't prevent clear and obvious evidence that the catch was made. The question should be this: Is it clear and obvious, given that movement is permitted, that Washington made the catch?

Most would say he did. If so, the ruling on the field should have been changed from incompletion to touchdown.

#2874. 49ers’ Colton McKivitz carted off after gruesome leg injury on 'Sunday Night Football'

The San Francisco 49ers lost their starting right tackle Colton McKivitz to a gruesome injury during their Week 11 "Sunday Night Football" game against the Minnesota Vikings.

McKivitz was injured after blocking on a Christian McCaffrey run in the latter half of the second quarter. The 30-year-old tackle was rolled up in a pile as McCaffrey was tackled and as McKivitz came out of the pile, his left foot was facing the wrong direction.

McKivitz's 49ers teammates were immediately upset looking at the tackle's injury. Christian McCaffrey fell to a knee looking at him while Trent Williams was shown on the broadcast looking dismayed, and he forcibly spiked his helmet into the ground.

McKivitz was quickly tended to by medical staff on the field and was carted into the locker room as a result of his injury. Swing tackle Vederian Lowe checked into the game to replace him.

49ers coach Kyle Shanahan provided an update about McKivitz's status after the game. He told reporters McKivitz suffered a fractured and dislocated left ankle and will miss the remainder of the season because of it, per ESPN's Nick Wagoner.

McKivitz entered play Sunday having played all 563 snaps for the 49ers offense, one of two San Francisco linemen to do that along with left guard Connor Colby.

McKivitz was injured while blocking on a Christian McCaffrey run. McCaffrey was tackled on the play and the veteran running back landed directly on the back of McKivitz's left leg.

McKivitz remained down on the field after the play and eventually rolled out of the pile, revealing that his left foot was facing the wrong way.

Vederian Lowe is McKivitz's backup. The five-year veteran was originally selected by the Vikings in the fifth round of the 2022 NFL Draft and has appeared in 55 career games with 25 starts, all of which came with the New England Patriots.

Lowe spent the previous three seasons with the Patriots and was a part of the team that made it to Super Bowl 60. Now, he will be tasked with starting in place of McKivitz with the 30-year-old right tackle set to miss the rest of the season.

The 49ers have four offensive tackles on their 53-man roster. Below is a look at the pecking order within the unit.

Cruz, a fifth-round rookie out of Kansas, is inactive for Sunday's game but will likely be active in future games as the team's swing tackle with McKivitz sidelined.

The 49ers also have Foster Sarell, who has appeared in 41 games (four starts) with the Los Angeles Chargers, and Isaac Alarcon of the International Player Pathway available on their practice squad to help man the position.

Additionally, San Francisco has Austen Pleasants, who appeared in 15 games for them last season with one start, on IR with a return designation. It isn't clear when he will be ready to return, but he could also be an option to replenish the team's tackle depth.

This story will be updated as more information becomes available.

#2873. Joe Mazzulla downplays disastrous third straight loss: ‘We played well enough’

For the third straight time, the Boston Celtics went out like a dud, collapsing against the two-win Sacramento Kings on Saturday night without Jayson Tatum, Derrick White and Mitchell Robinson. But head coach Joe Mazzulla was rather dismissive afterward.

"I think we played well enough to win the game," Mazzulla told reporters, per CLNS Media, after Boston's 97-96, "and they made more shots than we did, and made a couple of more plays down the stretch. You take a look at the process of the game, we played well and lost. So that's what you take from [it] and move on to tomorrow."

Mazzulla turned to Paul George and Payton Pritchard to lead the pack. They shared the floor with Sam Hauser, Hugo González and Neemias Queta in Boston's starting lineup. Yet the team's strongest and most efficient contribution didn't come from any of them. It came from 39-year-old guard Mike Conley, who had made just four appearances for Boston entering the night, averaging eight minutes per game before receiving 18 minutes off the bench in Sacramento.

Conley scored 19 points, converting his first six consecutive shot attempts, while adding four rebounds, three assists and two steals. From the looks of it, Conley had himself a heroic, game-saving performance in the works, good enough to get the Celtics back in the column. He was active, up to speed and turned back the clock to his vintage Memphis Grizzlies days, even though he sat on the bench for the entire first and third quarters.

Nov 21, 2026; Sacramento, California, USA; Boston Celtics forward Paul George (13) reacts during the third quarter against the Sacramento Kings at Golden 1 Center. Mandatory Credit: Justine Willard-Imagn Images | IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Had it not been for Conley's out-of-the-blue scoring outburst, the Kings might've run the Celtics out of the building.

"He kept us in it," Mazzulla said. "Tried to finish the game with him as much as we could. Thought he was great on both ends of the floor and tonight's the reason why you have a guy like that on your roster, and he gave us a chance to win tonight."

Conley stood tall as Boston's lone bright spot, tying George for the team's lead in scoring while finishing as Boston's plus-minus leader (+17).

The Celtics performed well below their standard, underscoring the impact Tatum's absence had against a Kings team that leads the Western Conference in turnovers (17.5), boasts the NBA's second-lowest-scoring offense (106.8 points) and had won just one of its first 15 games entering Saturday night.

Without their superstar, the wheels fell off the wagon.

In the third quarter, Mazzulla's group got outscored 35-13 and went 0-for-13 from three.

With back-to-back rest days, the underperformance was all the more inexcusable.

Boston finished the night shooting 33.7 percent from the field, going 34-for-101. They shot 22.4 percent from 3-point range, going 13-for-58 and setting a franchise record for most misses (45) in a single game. At the free-throw line, they went 15-for-25, adding further fuel to the fire.

George went 6-for-21, Pritchard went 4-for-17 and Baylor Scheierman shot 2-for-11.

Coming off consecutive losses to the Orlando Magic and Denver Nuggets, this was their ugliest dud by far.

In the fourth quarter, the Celtics traded baskets with Sacramento as the clock ticked down. Queta hit a go-ahead 3-pointer from the corner with 3:47 remaining, only for Kings rookie Darius Acuff Jr. to drain the equalizer, also from beyond the arc. George grabbed an offensive rebound and finished the putback layup with 49 seconds left. Acuff answered by drawing a foul and sinking both free throws at the other end. Pritchard sank a clutch fadeaway jumper to give Boston a 96-95 lead with 12 seconds remaining. Then the Celtics failed to protect the glass on the ensuing possession.

Acuff challenged González off the inbound, drove past him and missed his right-handed layup. But Sacramento's Keegan Murray leaped over Devin Carter to secure the offensive rebound and finish the putback, giving the Kings a 97-96 lead and leaving Boston with just five seconds for redemption.

For the game, Pritchard was handed the ball against Murray but failed to create enough space for a clean look, capping an already miserable performance with a wasted opportunity.

Pritchard finished with nine points and missed all eight of his 3-point attempts, which he owned up to.

"It's frustrating," Pritchard told reporters. "I just got to play better. I gotta do more, so a lot of that is on me in the third quarter and stuff like that. So it's my fault for that one."

Pritchard on the final possession: "Obviously didn't get to a good enough spot, and [Murray] played good defense. But probably need to try to find a better look, though."

The Celtics have lost three consecutive regular-season games for the first time since opening last season with three straight defeats. They suffered their most humbling loss yet, deservedly getting served their lunch by arguably the worst team in the NBA.

"I thought we competed at a high level through just a drought offensively where we couldn't make shots," Mazzulla said. "And again, you have to stick to the process of it. I thought we played well enough to win, and we just didn't. They made a few more plays than we did."

Winning and playing "well enough" to win aren't mutually exclusive. However, the Celtics didn't meet the threshold necessary to leave Sacramento victorious. Their result was entirely warranted.

#2867. Giants-Jaguars: Instant grades after New York’s victory over Jacksonville

The New York Giants defeated the now 7-3 Jacksonville Jaguars on Sunday to advance to 6-3-1. New York controlled the game, and Jacksonville was sloppy and undisciplined for much of the game. Still, the Giants' inability to put down an opponent after establishing a lead caused some fear in the second half. New York was up 20-6 at halftime, and was receiving the ball, but a missed Dominic Zvada field goal and a fumble by Cam Skattebo set an unfortunate tone for the offense throughout the second half.

New York went three-and-out three times in the second half, and four-and-out once. The offense continues to struggle, but the defense responded well. New York went 4 of 15 on third down and finished with just 238 yards of offense, but 13 Jacksonville penalties, two turnovers, and constant pressure on Trevor Lawrence were enough for New York to earn the victory. Here are the immediate grades from the Giants' sixth win of the season

The Giants offense struggled again. They were particularly bad in the second half. J.J. McCarthy finished 18 of 38 for 173 yards with a touchdown — a bad 4.6 yards per completion. McCarthy also missed some big-time throws to Darnell Mooney. He did have 33 yards on the ground and came up with a critical 19-yard pass to Malachi Fields to seal the victory after struggling throughout the second half; he went seven straight throws with an incompletion in the second half. It wasn't pretty, but the offense did enough to get the win.

Jameis Winston handled the red zone duties on the Giants' first touchdown drive. He checked out of a run and threw a bad ball to Isaiah Likely that could have been caught. The Giants went for it on fourth down, and Winston scored on the tush push.

New York struggled to consistently run the football. They averaged just 3.1 yards per carry, and that's beefed up by McCarthy's 4.7 YPC. Cam Skattebo — who had a costly fumble in the second half — had 14 carries for just 34 yards, but did catch three passes for 14 yards. Devin Singletary received snaps to spell Skattebo, but neither running back did much on the ground. The pass protection from Skattebo, though, was good, from what I could tell, once again.

The Giants' receivers only had 173 collective yards; 55 were to Malik Nabers, who secured seven of 12 targets with a touchdown. Malachi Fields caught a key 19-yard dig route that helped the Giants secure the victory, but the offense — in general — was quiet for much of the game. We did see Jalin Hyatt catch one pass for 4 yards and Xavier Gipson had a catch for 6 yards. Darnell Mooney created a lot of separation, but could not secure any of his five targets — McCarthy missed him badly on a deep post route.

The Giants tight ends were not heavily involved. Isaiah Likely caught one pass for 35 yards, and Chris Manhertz caught one ball for 14 yards. Theo Johnson had a clutch 15-yard grab over the middle of the field on third down, but Skattebo fumbled on the next play.

The Giants offensive line struggled early against Jacksonville; J.J. McCarthy faced two free rushers on the first two plays of the game. The pass blocking stabilized, and McCarthy was only sacked once, albeit there were too many free rushers. The offensive line struggled to create space at the point of attack, which is an annoying reality that has persisted for much of the season. Overall, the offensive line was less than stellar.

D.J. Reader picked up a first-quarter sack of Trevor Lawrence after a game shared with Abdul Carter, who spun back inside to help Reader finish the sack. New York's defense was STOUT and shut down Jacksonville's rushing attack. The defensive performance was an A+ overall, with Reader highlighted for four tackles and a sack, and C.J. Okoye with one STOP on early downs. Shelby Harris contributed three tackles.

Abdul Carter told the world to WATCH and he SHOWED UP with a big-time first quarter sack and a second down sack in the second quarter, as well as some valuable pressures. He timed the snap magnificently and strung moves together all game. Carter was sensational. However, Jacksonville figured out his timing in the fourth quarter, and drew an encroachment penalty.

Kayvon Thibodeaux was flying around the field and making important assisted tackles in short-yardage situations. He had a free run — as Dennard Wilson is one to dial up — at Trevor Lawrence, but could not get him on the ground. Dawuane Smoot had a sack negated, but continues to be a solid practice squad call-up.

The Giants linebackers were flying all over the field. Arvell Reese remains a heavy hammer that punishes offensive players. Micah McFadden missed a key fourth-down tackle on the Jaguars' first offensive drive, but he made several tackles throughout the game, including a few short-yardage stops. McFadden had Lawrence tee'd up multiple times throughout the game but struggled to get him down to the deck. McFadden was dinged up at the end of the game after missing a tackle in space against Chris Rodriguez Jr. Both McFadden and Arvell Reese had seven tackles; Zaire Barnes saw snaps and had four tackles.

The Giants used multiple cornerbacks in the game, as Greg Newsome II and Tae Banks missed time during the contest. Tae Banks made an early impact by intercepting Trevor Lawrence on the Jaguars' second offensive drive. Dru Phillips had a pair of HUGE pass deflections: one on a fourth-and-one route in the red zone and the other on third-and-goal with 3:43 left in the game, which prompted Cam Little's fourth field goal. He did, though, miss a few tackles. Phillips did surrender a third-and-twenty on a deep corner versus Washington.

Paulson Adebo received snaps after Newsome left and did not return; he made a great play on the football in Cover-4 on a deep post to Parker Washington on a first-down shot that went INC. And then, on the following play, Paulson Adebo intercepted Trevor Lawrence on a possible miscommunication with a Jaguars receiver.

Jevon Holland set a tone early by dislodging the football from Parker Washington. Holland was around the line of scrimmage making tackles, and he almost came away with an interception in the end zone on Jacksonville's second drive. Holland led the team with nine tackles, and Ar'Darious Washington had three.

Dominic Zvada is frustrating. He made a kick from the right hash! It started the Giants scoring, and then the rookie booted a 54-yard field goal at the end of the first half – AGAIN – from the right hash. It's a positive development for the rookie kicker. However, Zvada then missed a 32-yard field goal from the right hash to start the third quarter, and made his fourth kick. He remains inconsistent and borderline unreliable.

Jordan Stout averaged 60 yards per punt. He is very good at what he does. Xavier Gipson handled one punt and returned it for 27 yards, while also looking capable on his kickoff returns. The kick coverage failed on the first kickoff of the game, but Tyler Nubin made his presence known multiple times on coverage plays — he finished the game with three tackles and is becoming one of the best special teams players on the team.

#2866. Knicks 97, Trail Blazers 96: “We found a way to get a win”

If you wanted to explain to a new sports fan what the term "tale of two halves" means, just show them the tape from last night. The game started with a classic Jalen Brunson takeover that saw him go for 19 points, four rebounds, and two assists while shooting 6-7 from the field and 5-5 from three. And the Knicks did an amazing job playing off of his confidence and gravity en route to 40 first-quarter points. New York was playing with pace; the ball movement and player movement were constant and crisp, and everybody got in on the action. For a moment or two, it was reminiscent of the Knicks' Mother's Day massacre of the 76ers in the playoffs just months ago.

New York ended the first quarter with nine three-pointers while missing just two. Aside from Brunson's aforementioned five made threes, Mikal Bridges, Andre Drummond, Shamet, and McBride all added one each. And besides the nine threes, the Knicks had two dunks and one mid-range jump shot by Brunson. It was one of the most analytically beautiful shot charts anyone has ever seen.

The Trail Blazers, on the other hand, managed to score just 23 points in the first quarter. Deni Avdija did get off to a nice start with eight points, and Damian Lillard chipped in six of his own that accounted for more than half of their team's points. Toumani Camara, who was Trail Blazers head coach Micah Nori's answer to trying to stop Brunson, started off the game cold; Cidy Sissoko went scoreless, and starting center Donovan Clingan added just three points.

The second quarter didn't produce the same kind of exhilarating offense as the Knicks scored "just" 26 points. But even with the shooting seeing some expected negative regression, the Knicks still won the quarter by six as the Trail Blazers' offense limped its way to a 20-point quarter. The Knicks did struggle a bit with Brunson out as a Kolek-led second unit got some quality looks but just could not create advantages on a consistent basis. But as Knicks fans have said plenty of times over the last couple of years, thank god for Shamet. The veteran guard came up huge throughout the entire game as Brunson's much-needed sidekick. Shamet scored 13 of his 20 points in the second quarter and helped the Knicks take a 23-point lead into halftime.

But as noted earlier, the second half looked like a completely different ball game. The Knicks played one of their worst offensive quarters of the season, managing to score just 15 total points. Some of that was the Trail Blazers amping up the defense and coming out playing much more energized and more physical. But as head coach Mike Brown alluded to after the game, the Knicks looked like they were stuck in mud. There was significantly less movement overall. The ball was no longer zipping around the perimeter. Players were stagnant. And they no longer had the shooting touch to mask their lackluster offensive execution and subpar process. The Trail Blazers offense scored 29 points in the period and just like that, the lead was cut down to just nine points.

The Knicks often like to keep their fans on the edge of their seats, and Saturday night was no exception. Instead of dominating the third quarter with the same type of execution and intensity that they started the game with and taking the Trail Blazers out of the game, it was now down to one quarter. And you could tell early on in the final period that this wasn't going to be an easy win for the Knicks to pull out.

The Trail Blazers now had the confidence and the momentum. Lillard had struggled for much of the game, but he hit some key shots. Clingan was now dominating the paint and was on his way to a 22-point, 13-rebound double-double. Avdija, despite the Knicks generally playing good defense on him, was getting going. And that was happening all while the Knicks' offensive struggles continued. The Trail Blazers continued their upped intensity and were doing a much better job sticking with Shamet. Brunson, who was starting to look fatigued, was missing shots he usually makes. And both of those things were made much worse by the fact that Josh Hart, who had done a lot of nice non-scoring things in the first half, wasn't looking to shoot at all.

But there's a reason everyone calls the NBA a make-or-miss league. With the Trail Blazers taking a two-point lead in the final seconds thanks to a clutch three by Lillard, Bridges, who has slowly but surely turned the page on what was a disastrous first few weeks of the season, made a game-winning three against the Trail Blazers for the second time in three seasons.

Had he missed that shot, Saturday night's loss would have turned into the worst loss of the season. Yes, Karl-Anthony Towns and OG Anunoby were sidelined. But to lose that game at home after how the first half started would've been a gut-wrenching loss. Instead, Bridges makes the shot and the Knicks win by one, and it's the Trail Blazers who are now on the wrong side of what was objectively a fun NBA game. Portland finds themselves regretting how they came out in the first half, and Avdija has to be thinking about the three free throws he missed in the fourth quarter.

The Knicks' Saturday night win over the Trail Blazers was emblematic of the Knicks as a whole. Sometimes, they look like one of the best offenses of all time. Sometimes, they can't concoct anything better than a stagnant possession that leads to a Brunson isolation with seconds left. But as Brown said during his postgame presser, they "found a way to get a win".

As this team has proven time and time again, regardless of who is playing and who is out, regardless of who the opponent is, this team will find ways to stay in the game, and more often than not, they'll come out on top. It isn't always easy. It isn't always pretty. But a win is a win.

#2864. Social Security benefits are rising in 2027, but other changes could affect what you keep

Whether you're working or retired, changes to Social Security in 2027 could affect your finances.

Starting in January, millions of program beneficiaries will get a 3.6% increase to their benefits, thanks to the annual cost-of-living adjustment. But rising Medicare premiums could chip away at the increase — and as beneficiaries' incomes rise, that may affect the taxes they owe and their eligibility for a new senior deduction.

Meanwhile, a higher taxable earnings cap means some workers will pay Social Security taxes on more income, reducing their paycheck.

Here's a breakdown of what to expect in the new year.

Social Security's limit for payroll taxes is adjusted every year to reflect the average wage index.

In 2027, the taxable maximum will go up to $192,300 in earnings, up from $184,500 in 2026. Workers and employers each pay a 6.2% payroll tax on earnings up to that limit — or 12.4% for self-employed workers. Medicare taxes of 1.45% for both workers and employers are not subject to a limit.

In 2027, workers who earn $192,300 or more will pay $11,922.60 in total Social Security payroll taxes, according to the Social Security Administration, while self-employed workers will pay $23,845.20.

"If you're somebody whose income is over what the new threshold is going to be, this increase in the threshold results in a tax increase to you," said Tim Steffen, director of advanced planning at financial services firm Baird.

The 3.6% Social Security cost-of-living adjustment for 2027 is slated to increase retirement benefits by about $75 per month on average, according to the Social Security Administration.

The annual adjustment is the largest boost to benefits since 2023 due to rising inflation. The COLA averaged 2.6% from 2000 to 2026, according to the Senior Citizens League, a nonpartisan senior group.

Around 72 million Social Security beneficiaries will see benefit increases, according to the agency.

The maximum benefit for a worker at full retirement age — when individuals become eligible for 100% of the benefits they've earned — will rise to $4,188 per month in 2027, up from $4,152 per month in 2026, according to the federal agency. To be eligible for the maximum benefit at full retirement age — typically age 66 to 67 — you generally have to earn up to the taxable threshold for each of your working years factored into the calculation.

How much of the COLA increase Social Security beneficiaries see depends in part on their Medicare premiums, which may be deducted directly from benefit checks.

Monthly rates are poised to increase for both Medicare Part B, which covers medically necessary services and preventive care, and Part D, which provides prescription drug coverage.

The standard monthly Medicare Part B premium will be $209.40 in 2027 — a $6.50 increase from $202.90 in 2026, according to the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services.

"It's not often that we see a Part B [premium] increase that is lower than the COLA," Mary Johnson, an independent Social Security and Medicare policy analyst, said of the 3.2% increase. The last time that happened was in 2023, she said.

Medicare Part D beneficiaries may also pay higher premiums in 2027.

Assuming enrollees keep the same plan, the average enrollment-weighted monthly Part D premium may be $51 in 2027, up 50% from $34 in 2026, according to projections from KFF, a provider of health policy research and polling. That is higher than CMS' projections, which forecast the national average premium for standalone Part D plans will rise about $1, to $36, in 2027 from $35 in 2026.

Standard monthly premiums for Part B and plan-specific premiums for Part D apply to individuals who have $112,000 or less in modified adjusted gross income, or $224,000 for married couples. Beneficiaries with higher income pay larger premiums because of income-related monthly adjustment amounts, which affect 9% of Medicare Part B and Part D beneficiaries, according to CMS.

With bigger Social Security benefits, more households may find that some portion of benefits is taxable — though a new senior deduction may help provide some relief to those who are eligible.

Social Security benefits may be subject to federal income taxes for certain individuals. The thresholds at which those taxes are applied are not adjusted from year to year.

Whether or not benefits are taxed is determined by a formula known as combined income — the sum of adjusted gross income, tax-exempt interest income and half of Social Security benefits:

Individuals who are subject to taxes may opt to withhold taxes from their Social Security benefit checks, rather than face a potential bill during tax filing season.

Separately, a temporary senior deduction of up to $6,000 for individuals ages 65 and over may help to indirectly reduce taxes on Social Security benefits.

That deduction, part of the One Big Beautiful Bill Act in 2025, helps reduce retirees' taxable income, according to Steffen. It's in place through 2028. Because the break phases out for individuals with modified adjusted gross income starting at $75,000 — and for married couples with $150,000 — it's important to watch your income if you're close to those limits, Steffen said.

"Keep in mind that if you're over 65, you might qualify for this deduction," Steffen said. "But if you let your income get too big, you might lose the deduction."

Moreover, any extra income that causes you to lose the deduction may also come with its own taxes, making it more expensive, according to Steffen.

"What we've been telling our clients is that tax planning is a lot more complicated than it used to be, and you can't do it on the back of an envelope anymore," he said. "You need to use good tools that can do good calculations for you."

Individuals who claim Social Security retirement benefits before their full retirement age and who continue to work may be subject to withholdings.

In 2027, individuals under full retirement age may earn up to $25,560 per year, up from $24,480 in 2026, without adjustments to their Social Security benefits. For every $2 in earnings above that limit, $1 in Social Security benefits will be withheld.

Importantly, those withholdings are not lost completely. Once a beneficiary reaches full retirement age, the withheld money will be credited to their benefits over their remaining lifespan.

In the year individuals reach full retirement age, the retirement earnings test limit is higher — $67,920 per year in 2027, up from $65,160 per year in 2026. For every $3 in earnings above that limit, $1 in Social Security benefits is withheld.

Starting the month a beneficiary reaches full retirement age, no earnings are withheld.

#2861. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2858. Week 6 Fantasy Basketball Week Ahead: Nuggets pick up two big wins; Dylan Harper and Jaylen Brown injured

With Week 6 on the horizon, fantasy managers have another day off to account for, as the entire NBA takes Thanksgiving Day off. While the holiday hasn't wreaked havoc on many team schedules, a few may not be the best options as managers craft their rosters for the week. Week 5 delivered a few more historic performances (thank you, Victor Wembanyama and James Harden), while Chicago, Philadelphia and San Antonio lost starters, and the Warriors were hit hard by Stephen Curry's absence.

- Nuggets pick up big wins over Rockets, Celtics: Denver's surprising November 16 loss to the Suns dropped their record to 7-5, which certainly isn't bad, but the result was one that few expected. David Adelman's team rebounded nicely, picking up home wins over the Celtics and Rockets ahead of their Week 5 finale in Chicago on Sunday. While Nikola Jokić and Jamal Murray led the way offensively as expected, Aaron Gordon and DeMar DeRozan's improved play stood out. In those victories, Gordon totaled 31 points (11-of-19 FGs, 8-of-11 FTs), 11 rebounds and four assists. While the three-point shooting remains an issue, Gordon's overall efficiency and production improved, a welcome development.

As for DeRozan, he's struggled some to fit in with his new team. However, he came up big against the Celtics, including a run early in the fourth quarter in which he got the Nuggets into the penalty by himself. Denver outscored Boston 15-2 at the foul line in the fourth, which was much needed, as the team was a plus-4 (25-21) for the quarter in a game they would win by four points. Against Boston and Houston, DeRozan totaled 33 points (9-of-22 FGs, 14-of-15 FTs), four rebounds and four assists. Neither Gordon nor DeRozan posted elite numbers in the wins over Houston and Boston. However, both showed signs of turning things around after an uneven start to the season.

- Spurs lose Dylan Harper for 1-3 weeks: Not only did the Spurs' 12-game winning streak end on November 17 against the Pistons, but they also lost a key player. Harper was injured late in the fourth quarter when he slipped while attempting to catch a pass, suffering a hip strain that's expected to sideline him anywhere from one to three weeks.

As has been the case when the Spurs were down another perimeter player this season, Julian Champagnie has returned to the starting lineup. In wins over the Clippers and Pelicans, he totaled 29 points (11-of-24 FGs), seven rebounds, four assists, one block and seven three-pointers. Champagnie stands to be most valuable to fantasy managers needing three-point production.

Also of note from those two games without Harper was Stephon Castle's play. While he committed eight turnovers against the Clippers, the third-year guard totaled 40 points (14-of-24 FGs, 5-of-5 FTs), nine rebounds, 18 assists, four steals, seven three-pointers and 10 turnovers. Although his field-goal percentage has decreased a few points (43.7 compared to 47.1 in 2025-26), Castle has kept the turnovers in check this season, with his average down to 2.4 per game (from 3.2 tpg last season)

- 76ers lose Jaylen Brown (hamstring): After playing 33 minutes in the 76ers' November 16 win over the Nets, Brown was diagnosed with a strained left hamstring that sidelined him for Philadelphia's final two games of Week 5. He was supposed to be re-evaluated ahead of Saturday's game against the Bucks, but the team released no news. Brown, who did some individual work at Friday's practice, is day-to-day until the 76ers provide a firm timeline. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope started the two games Brown missed, recording totals of 19 points (7-of-11 FGs), nine rebounds, three assists, two steals and five three-pointers. The veteran guard is a decent option for deep-league managers needing three-point production, with steals being a bonus.

Also, VJ Edgecombe played 43 and 38 minutes in the two games Philadelphia played without Brown to end Week 5, recording totals of 40 points (13-of-27 FGs, 9-of-9 FTs), 11 rebounds, 12 assists, four steals, one block and five three-pointers. While his shot attempts have increased slightly in Brown's absence, Edgecombe's increased playing time stands out and likely raises the second-year guard's fantasy ceiling in the short term.

- Raptors' injury woes continue as Barrett, CMB go down: Already playing without Kawhi Leonard, whose knee injury has kept him out since October 23, Toronto lost RJ Barrett (hamstring) and Collin Murray-Boyles (concussion protocol) during Week 5. Barrett will be re-evaluated in two weeks, while Murray-Boyles' timeline depends on how soon he's cleared to resume full activity. Those injuries have freed up opportunities for multiple players, including Jamison Battle and first-round pick Allen Graves.

In games against the Wizards and Pistons, Battle totaled 29 points (11-of-21 FGs), 10 rebounds, one steal and seven three-pointers. While he started against Washington and played 33 minutes, Battle was used in a reserve role in the November 20 win over Detroit, playing 20 minutes. As for Graves, he had a career night against Detroit, finishing with 20 points (6-of-9 FGs, 6-of-6 FTs), 10 rebounds, one assist, three steals, one block and two three-pointers. While he came off the bench for his lone season at Santa Clara, Graves' advanced numbers were a big reason he was selected in the first round. Like Murray-Boyles, he has the tools needed to be a reliable category-league option for fantasy managers.

- Anthony Edwards (knee) reveals that he has no return timeline: Sidelined since the final week of October after undergoing an arthroscopic procedure on his right knee, Edwards met with the media ahead of the Timberwolves' November 19 loss to the Knicks. The Timberwolves star said he doesn't have a firm return timeline, so it could go longer than the initially reported two-month timeline. Ayo Dosunmu has been the replacement in the starting lineup, and he's performed well, averaging 19.7 points, 4.5 rebounds, 3.2 assists, 1.6 steals and 2.4 three-pointers in 13 starts.

Jaden McDaniels is enjoying the best individual season of his NBA career, while LaMelo Ball and Jonathan Kuminga have fit in well with his new team. Since losing Edwards, the Timberwolves have gone 9-4 and looked like a team more than capable of holding down the fort until their star returns. The one concern for Minnesota may be Rudy Gobert, who has continued to struggle offensively. His scoring average of 7.6 points per game is the lowest since his rookie season (2013-14).

- Austin Reaves (heel) should return...but when?: Reaves has missed the Lakers' last four games with a left heel contusion, but he could be back "soon." Ahead of Friday's win over the Kings, Lakers head coach JJ Redick said that he hoped to get Reaves back within the next two games. If Reaves can't play on Monday against the Jazz, the next possible return day would be the day after Thanksgiving against the Spurs in San Antonio. Quentin Grimes has performed well as the replacement in the starting lineup, hitting 10 three-pointers and dropping 47 points in an overtime win over the Hornets, but the Lakers also need Reaves back to ease some of the pressure on Luka Dončić as a playmaker.

- Stephen Curry (oblique) to be re-evaluated: Injured on November 11, Curry was given a re-evaluation timeline of two weeks, which falls in the middle of Week 6. Obviously, he isn't guaranteed to return to the court immediately, but the Warriors desperately need their star guard back. Not only has Golden State lost all four games that Curry's missed, but they also haven't been competitive. During this five-game losing streak, which began with the game against the Rockets in which Curry was injured, the Warriors have led for a total of 46 seconds and have four wire-to-wire defeats.

While Yaxel Lendeborg has done what he can in Curry's absence, making him the primary scoring option puts him in an incredibly difficult spot. Lendeborg's efficiency has suffered, while Brandin Podziemski has scored 15 points or less in each of the Warriors' last four games.

- Evan Mobley is expected to be re-evaluated: Mobley has been out since the Cavaliers' November 11 win over the Knicks with a toe injury, and on November 16 it was reported that he would be re-evaluated in 7-10 days. Given the timeline, he should be re-evaluated early in Week 6, with Cleveland playing three games next week. They'll play road games against the Bulls (Monday) and Knicks (Wednesday) before hosting the Heat on Sunday. Tyrese Proctor initially moved into the starting lineup after Peyton Watson was sidelined for the November 14 win over the Mavericks, and he's remained in that role since Watson returned.

In four starts, Proctor averaged 13.5 points, 4.0 rebounds, 3.8 assists and 2.3 three-pointers per game, shooting 47.7 percent from the field and 60 percent from the foul line. It should be noted that the first game of that stretch, the win over Dallas, was played without Cleveland's entire starting five. Proctor's production has decreased game by game, but Monday's matchup with the Bulls could be a good one for him.

- Norman Powell (hamstring) to be re-evaluated during Week 6: Powell has been diagnosed with a "mild" strain of his right hamstring and is expected to be re-evaluated during Week 6. The Bulls open their Week 6 at home against the Cavaliers on Monday, so that game can likely be ruled out. Chicago finishes the week with games against the Hornets (Wednesday) and the Pacers (Saturday), so fantasy managers may not get anything out of Powell.

With Isaac Okoro already in the starting lineup due to Caleb Wilson's broken hand, Buddy Hield could be the player who steps in for Powell. The veteran guard has scored in double figures in four of his last five games, and he's made at least two three-pointers in five straight games. Hield doesn't qualify as a must-add player, but he's worth watching in the Bulls' Week 5 finale against the Nuggets on November 22.

- Atlanta, Dallas and Washington have two-game weeks: The Hawks, Mavericks and Wizards play two games in Week 6. However, while Dallas and Washington will each play a game before Thanksgiving, both of Atlanta's games will be played after the holiday. The Hawks have been without Kingston Flemings (back) and CJ McCollum (calf) recently, with the latter not expected to be re-evaluated for another three weeks. Aaron Wiggins has moved into the starting lineup, while the playmaking responsibilities have been shared among Nickeil Alexander-Walker, Dyson Daniels and Jalen Johnson. Hopefully, the time off is enough for Flemings to return if he can't play in Atlanta's Week 5 finale against the Pistons on November 22.

1. Will Jaylen Brown be able to return during Week 6?

2. Will Tyrese Haliburton (hamstring) return before the start of December?

3. How close is Paolo Banchero (ankle) to a return, and how will it impact Franz Wagner?

#2853. Rockets must choose carefully in strange 2027 NBA Draft

If the 2026-27 NBA season ended today, the Houston Rockets would have roughly a 64% chance of landing a top-five pick.

I thought it was roughly 80%. The Brooklyn Nets and Phoenix Suns both have 40%-ish odds; add them together, 40%.

I was, as is so often the case, corrected on X. If each independent event has a 40% chance of happening, each has a 60% chance of not happening. So anyway, long and short, accounting for that, it's about 64%.

Remember when Rafael Stone said not to judge the James Harden trade until 2030? Well, we're almost in 2027, and it feels pretty safe to credit Stone's vision. The Rockets are 12-4; it's nearly certain that they'll have a lottery pick (and probably two) this summer, and there's a pretty good chance one of those picks lands in the top five. Life is good.

There's always a catch, isn't there? The Rockets draft one of the best centers of all time in the same draft that the best player of all time is drafted in. They change NBA offense, but another team changes it even more. Whatever.

The catch is that this draft is widely viewed as weak. If you've researched it, you've likely come to the same conclusion. There's Kansas' Tyran Stokes, and then…yeah.

At least, the list of (nearly) surefire NBA stars begins and ends with Stokes. That doesn't mean there aren't quality players in the draft:

It means Rafael Stone will have to dig for them.

Caveat One: We're early in the draft cycle. It's entirely possible that someone whose name isn't in the most thorough Excel file on the most obsessive aspiring internet scout's laptop.

Caveat Two: I wrote a whole piece about how the Rockets should be looking at Baba Oladotun, and he's averaging a red-hot 4, 2, and 1. Have your grain of salt on hand.

Right now, I'm fixated on two particular prospects. Illinois' Quentin Coleman is a 6'4″ combo guard. I'm convinced he's got a distinctly positive wingspan – I'd estimate 6'8″ – but Google has no idea. He's averaging 17.8 points, 6.8 rebounds, and 4.4 assists while shooting 55.6% from the field and 37.0% from three-point range on 5.4 attempts per game.

The cool thing about Coleman is that he's a lead ball-handler who plays with the energy of a role player. He reminds me of a young Lonzo Ball, with maybe a bit more on-ball juice and a bit less defense.

The other is Ivan Kharchenkov, a 6'7″, 230-pound wing on Arizona. Kharchenkov almost entered the draft last year. He's an outstanding, aggressive perimeter defender with some ancillary skills: He can pass and dribble some. The problem with Kharchenkov has always been his low three-point volume, which never bodes well for an NBA role player.

This year, Kharchenkov is forcing us to ask a question: What if he isn't a role player? He's averaging 22.6 points, 8.0 rebounds, and 4.6 assists per game.

There are concerns about Kharchenkov. He's still shooting just 4.0 threes per game. It's also been suggested that his strength-based shot creation won't work in the NBA, where men are stronger. If he's forced to fend for himself, Kharchenkov tends to muscle into his defender with a few dribbles to create space for his mid-range.

Those are my guys. You may have your own. The broader point is this: Very few "bad" NBA drafts are as bad as they were perceived to be in hindsight. It does happen, but more often, there are stars hiding in plain sight. At this convergence of fortune and strategy, all the Rockets can do is choose carefully. It could be the difference between finding the would-be best team in the NBA's missing piece, and adding another dud to rot away in the 8th man spot until he's trade fodder.

For now, let's wait and see what the Draft Gods decide.

#2849. Trump says there could be a 'positive spin' from the latest FBI database hack

President Donald Trump suggested Saturday that there could be a bright spot in the latest incursion into the FBI computer database.

The president confirmed in an interview with Fox News that a system has been breached that houses information used by law enforcement at all levels to share criminal justice information.

The hackers accessed the National Criminal Information Center (NCIC), a database that includes FBI fingerprint records, wanted persons, violent felons, missing persons and related information, two people familiar with the matter told MS NOW. The database is restricted to law enforcement use but not classified and it is unclear what data was extracted.

CNN had first reported on the suspected hack, citing sources familiar with the issue.

In a wide-ranging interview on Fox, Trump said there could be upside from the news.

"Maybe we'll learn about the voter fraud, all the voter fraud that took place," he said. "I always like to put things on a positive spin."

This would be the second recent intrusion into the FBI system; the previous one came in September, when a group known as "ShinyHunters" broke into an FBI jobs website. The result compromised data on current and former FBI employees and shut down part of the agency's operations until the issue was resolved.

Trump said it's possible there could be information gleaned from the latest hack.

"I wonder who's doing it, because wouldn't it be great to learn about Russia, Russia, Russia?" he said. "People should hear about it, because it actually put our country in danger, and so maybe we'll learn things that we wouldn't have learned otherwise."

The FBI did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

#2846. ANALYSIS: Does Nets’ closing problem have solution?

The idea of having the best of both worlds sounds nice, right?

Unfortunately, in life, things typically don't work that way. There are trade-offs in just about everything. The frustrating part is that either choice feels wrong, especially in the NBA. But neither choice is exactly right, either.

It's an interesting conundrum, especially if you are the 2026-27 Brooklyn Nets.

Through 15 games, Brooklyn, sitting at 3-12, has somehow managed to lose five games by 15+ points. It's the most in the league. However, the squad has also dropped four games by five points or fewer.

That's what makes the Nets' start to the season so interesting. There have certainly been several blowouts, and the squad has only three wins, but there is also a chunk of games where Brooklyn was in it until the final buzzer. That includes Friday's tough 113-111 loss to the Orlando Magic.

According to Cleaning the Glass, Brooklyn has won 1.4 fewer games than their point differential would suggest, the fifth-worst mark in the league. Based on their -7.1 point differential, the Nets would be expected to have 4.4 wins through 15 games.

When those games get tight, the Nets just haven't been able to pull away consistently. Despite having the NBA's highest effective field goal percentage in the fourth quarter, their shooting drops off significantly in clutch situations — the final few minutes of games. The Nets are shooting just 37.2% from the field in those moments, ranking 23rd in the league. Their free-throw shooting has been even worse, sitting at 60%, the fourth-worst mark.

Subsequently, the Nets' winning percentage in clutch situations is just 20.0%, the third-lowest clip in the NBA. The biggest problem comes with closing games, not necessarily being competitive or keeping it close entering the fourth.

Is it coaching? Is it execution? Is it the lack of a closer? The Nets seem to be searching for those same answers themselves.

However, from afar, Jordi Fernández looks to be facing a deeper problem that could be out of control. There is only so much he can do with his rotations. Even on Friday night, he switched up a multitude of components in his minutes distribution, including starting Danny Wolf over Egor Dëmin, which ultimately panned out.

In large part, the overarching issue with Brooklyn comes back to the trade-off element. Want more scoring? There's a good chance you're sacrificing defense. Prioritize the other end? You probably have to sacrifice scoring.

Fernández isn't entirely absolved of blame, but the Nets lack legitimate two-way players. And when you're trying to win a game in the final minutes, particularly against more talented teams, that only makes it harder.

The Nets have been stuck in a tough predicament, trying to balance what matters most in late-game situations: offense or defense. That has cost them in multiple games.

At the end of the day, the Nets don't have an established closing lineup. And that's hard to overcome in today's NBA, especially when games slow down in the final few minutes, and there's little margin for error.

Think about some of the better teams in the league, some that Brooklyn has even played. When a game gets tight, there is a pretty solid understanding of who's getting the basketball. Most solid squads have that guy they trust in crucial situations to turn to when things start to break down.

First off, who is that guy for the Nets? Michael Porter Jr. is probably the closest thing. He has been their most dependable offensive weapon, but is he really that high-end takeover guy?

That's where the roster's lack of two-way versatility becomes even more problematic. When Fernández needs offense, he has to consider which players give Brooklyn the best chance to score. However, in many cases, that comes at the expense of defense. That's what's so frustrating with the state of the roster.

Unfortunately for the Nets, it's not as simple as just putting "the five best players" out on the court. It's simply not how the roster is constructed: eight players are on rookie contracts, and the team is the second-youngest in the NBA over the past 25 years, older than only last year's group.

The Nets lack a proven guy who can dominate games down the stretch. Obviously that comes over time, and for a rebuilding team like Brooklyn, a player like that doesn't emerge overnight. The hope is that Mikel Brown Jr. can become that guy, and the early returns on the rookie are promising so far, but he is still just 12 games into his NBA career while also being on a minutes restriction.

Individually, Brooklyn has pieces that can be useful. It's been promising to see growth from players like Nolan Traoré and Danny Wolf, looking to take legitimate second-year leaps. Julius Randle and MPJ add veteran experience and scoring punch. Then there is someone like Day'Ron Sharpe who has impressed while emerging as the team's starting big.

Those players can be useful. Collectively, though, it's tough to find a five-man combination that doesn't leave something to be desired.

For a young team, there is some encouragement in being competitive. Hanging with high-level teams down the stretch like the Boston Celtics and the New York Knicks is encouraging, but when will those tight games actually translate into wins? There is also a distinction between being competitive but losing and losing games that are supposed to be wins, especially against strong competition. The latter can't always be used as an excuse.

The head coach isn't completely out of this conversation. A few of his rotational decisions, even with the newer changes, have been questionable. Look no further than Noah Clowney.

Clowney, 22, is in the final year of his rookie contract. While he's still 22, he hasn't produced much consistently. That's been the biggest concern with the Alabama product since he entered the league.

Through 15 games, though, he hasn't done much to change that narrative. In 21 minutes per game, he is averaging 6.5 points, 4.2 rebounds, and 1.1 assists on 41.3% from the field.

Furthermore, he owns a team-low -107 plus/minus. For context, that's also a 17-percentage-point difference between him and the second-lowest-rated net: Mo Wagner at -90. Meanwhile, his 3-point percentage (27.7%) is also the lowest on the team among qualified shooters (minimum 3.0 attempts per game).

There have been some glimpses. He had back-to-back games against Memphis and New Orleans where he finished with at least 15 points and two 3s. Overall, though, not much has changed. The flashes are still there, but Clowney continues to struggle to find that level of consistency.

And that brings us back to Friday night, when he finished with zero points in 22 minutes, shooting 0-of-4 from the field, and recording a team-worst minus-12 rating. Yet, with the game on the line, he was still on the floor, defending Franz Wagner, who hit the game-winning shot over him.

Now Wagner had a tremendous game, finishing with 35 points, and his offensive ability is highly touted. However, putting Clowney out there in that situation was questionable to begin with.

If he isn't providing offensively, what exactly is the team gaining by keeping him out there? While he came away with a block and a steal, he hasn't been regarded as a plus defender, especially for his position and size, at 6'10" and 210 pounds. Most glaringly, his plus/minus rating was not only the lowest on Friday, but it's also the lowest among the whole team this year, and by a pretty wide margin.

Despite that, though, he continues to earn consistent minutes. He ranks eighth on the team in minutes per game (21.0), behind Randle (33.1), Porter (32.9), Dëmin (24.0), Sharpe (23.9), Wolf (23.0), Brown (22.9), and Traoré (22.3). So esentially, he's been the Nets' third player off the bench this season. That's a pretty important role.

Clearly, with him in that role and out on the court to close games, things haven't been working too well. That's where Fernández comes in. He needs to be willing to make more on-the-fly adjustments to the rotation, especially when things aren't working.

How about looking at players like Josh Minott and Drake Powell? Neither has received notable minutes this season out of garbage time. Neither is a proven NBA defender, but is it time to give them minutes to see what happens? It's worked with Danny Wolf.

After all, isn't that supposed to be part of a rebuilding season? Minott, 23, shot 39.5% from deep wth Brooklyn last year after being acquired at the trade deadline, and the organization saw enough to award him a two-year, $9 million extension in the offseason. At 6'8" and 205 pounds, he also has the tools to be a disruptor on the defensive end.

Powell, meanwhile, has spent much of the season in the G League so far with Brooklyn's affiliate, the Long Island Nets. He has played in five NBA games and was called up for Friday, although most of his minutes have come in garbage time. The North Carolina product boasts a 43-inch vertical and a 7-foot wingspan, giving him intriguing potential on offense and defense, notably at the point of attack. That was on display at points last year. In fact, his 91.9 defensive rating led the NBA last December, and the Nets actually went 7-4 that month.

Of course, the Nets have a free agency decision on Clowney coming up in the Summer. And of course, the Nets are bringing Powell along slowly. He was, after all, the biggest reach of the Flatbush 5. But, of course, the offense remains a work in progress. He shot just 28% from 3 during his rookie campaign and a painful 4-of-16 from that range in Summer League, including a 0-of-14 start.

There is also two-way forward Chaney Johnson, who played a firm role in the rotation at the start of the season, but his minutes have gradually disappeared. Johnson still lacks an established offensive game, but he can guard multiple positions on both ends of the floor.

Maybe Minott struggles. Maybe Powell shows that he isn't ready. Maybe Johnson doesn't improve much either. However, getting that understanding is important, especially when things aren't working.

And if one of them does emerge as a useful piece, particularly defensively, that would be huge for the roster's current outlook.

There may not be a perfect solution. At 3-12, though, the team needs to keep experimenting, especially off the bench. Fernández' decision regarding Dëmin and Wolf on Friday paid off; now it's important to see that same mindset carry over to bench minutes.

Given the organization's overall situation, there's much more to gain from trying something different than sticking with what hasn't worked.
